Here k0 is fixed and sufficiently large. To prove this, take arbitrarily late running records rn of
g(k) − α1 k for which g(rn )/rn > α2 . Such records exist: a time with g(k) > α2 k forces the preceding
running maximum to occur at a time r ≤ k with g(r) − α1 r > (α2 − α1 )k ≥ (α2 − α1 )r. The
maximizing times tend to infinity. Since P > ρ, the function g(k) − P k tends to −∞; let kn ≥ rn
maximize it on [rn , ∞). For k ≥ kn this gives the second inequality in (6.26). If rn ≤ k ≤ kn , it
gives g(k) ≤ g(kn ) + P (k − kn ) ≤ g(kn ) + α1 (k − kn ). For k ≤ rn , combine the record bound with
g(kn ) ≥ g(rn ) + P (kn − rn ), obtaining the first inequality again. The same last inequality gives
g(kn ) ≥ α2 kn .

                              dn = e−kn ,    mn = eg(kn ) ,       bn = dn mn .                   (6.27)

                                              bn

because bn ≥ e(α2 −1)kn , whereas log mn = O(kn ).
Compact profiles and a trivial pair. For any choice of real centers xn , introduce the rescaled shape
coordinate S = (s − xn − iH)/dn and fields

                              f (xn + iH + dn S)                     q(xn + iH + dn S)
                   Fn (S) =                      ,        Qn (S) =                     .         (6.29)
                                      mn                                    m2n

Their domains exhaust the lower half-plane. The envelopes give, with D = −ℑS,
                                                            (
                                                 1/2        D−α1 , D ≥ 1,
                         max{|Fn (S)|, |Qn (S)|        }≤                                        (6.30)
                                                            D−P , 0 < D ≤ 1,

on every fixed compact set for all large n. These bounds are uniform in all real centers. Montel’s
theorem gives subsequential holomorphic limits F, Q. The rescaled differential equation is

                                             Fn′′ = b2n Qn Fn .                                  (6.31)

Cauchy estimates bound the left side locally, so F Q = 0. Since the half-plane is connected, either
F ≡ 0 or Q ≡ 0. Every limit also satisfies the deeper bound D−α1 in (6.30).
    We claim that some sequence of centers, after passage to a subsequence, has F = Q = 0. Suppose
otherwise. Fix a small closed disk about −i inside the half-plane and take the supremum norms of
the two fields on it. Compactness then gives a constant c > 0 such that, for all centers and large n,
the larger of the two norms is at least c. Otherwise a sequence with both norms tending to zero
would have the zero pair as its limit everywhere, by analytic uniqueness. Purity and compactness
likewise imply that the smaller norm tends to zero uniformly over centers: any contrary sequence
would have a limiting pair with both norms positive. The norms depend continuously on the
center. Once their smaller value is less than c/3, the two possible dominant types give disjoint open
sets covering the connected circle of centers. Thus one type holds at every center. Passing to a
subsequence, it is the same type for every n.
    Suppose first that it is the F -type. All local limiting functions F obtained from arbitrary centers
are nonzero. This compact family has a uniform bound on the number of zeros in any fixed smaller
disk about −i, and, for every fixed small η > 0, a uniform positive lower bound away from the
η-neighborhoods of those zeros. Here is a direct compactness justification. A convergent subsequence
has a nonzero holomorphic limit; choose a slightly larger circle without zeros of that limit. The
argument principle bounds the number of zeros inside, and uniform convergence bounds the function
below on a smaller compact set away from the zeros. A failure of either uniform assertion would
produce a contrary convergent subsequence. The same assertions hold on the slightly enlarged
scaled neighborhoods needed to cover a narrow band about the row D = 1.
    Cover the physical periodic row at depth dn by O(d−1     n ) cells of size comparable to dn . Around
its nearby zeros remove disks of radii between ηdn and 2ηdn , with generic radii to avoid boundary
tangencies. Choose η sufficiently small in terms of the uniform local zero count. Every connected
cluster meeting the row then has a uniformly bounded number of disks and diameter much smaller
than dn . Indeed, a minimal chain escaping a fixed enlarged cell would require more zeros in that

                                                     66
cell than the uniform bound allows. Fill holes in the clusters and discard nested obstacles. Detour
the row along the outer boundary arcs of these clusters. The resulting closed contour Cn winds
once around the periodic cylinder, stays in a fixed narrow band about depth dn , and satisfies

                            length(Cn ) = O(1),            |f | ≥ cη mn    on Cn .                (6.32)

The length estimate follows because the total number of relevant zero disks is O(d−1      n ), each with
circumference O(dn ).

Indeed the horizontal mean of log |f | is convex as a function of height, by subharmonicity and

because log |f | is locally integrable. At these centers pass to a subsequence with Qn → Q ̸≡ 0.
    Near a point where Q ̸= 0, choose a unit complex number e and a fixed short segment in

                              u′′ = 2|eFn′ |2 + 2b2n ℜ(e2 Qn )|Fn |2 ≥ 2c′ b2n u.

justify comparison, subtract that solution; a positive interior maximum of the difference contradicts
w′′ − 2c′ b2n w ≥ 0. The same estimate holds uniformly on a small neighborhood of the chosen point.
     Choose a circle centered at −i, contained in the half-plane, and avoiding the discrete zero set
of Q. A finite cover by these neighborhoods gives |Fn | ≤ Ce−cbn on the circle, after changing c.
Maximum modulus gives the same bound at its center. Therefore

                               |f (xn + i(H − dn ))| ≤ Cmn e−cbn −→ 0

by (6.28), contradicting (6.33). Both common types are impossible, proving the existence of trivial
profiles.
A global width bound and a nontrivial limiting lift. Use the centers with the trivial pair in Theo-
rem 6.5(ii). The bound (6.30) is stronger on the coarse tail than its hypothesis, since α1 > 1; it may
be weakened to any exponent between 1/2 and 1. Since dn /bn = 1/mn , we obtain

                                     max{|Fn (−i)|, |Qn (−i)|1/2 } = 1.

   Fix a real T ̸= 0. Define, now as a function of the rescaled phase coordinate Y ,

                                  T Fn (A + hn u),    T 2 Qn (A + hn u).

   The stronger exponent α1 now improves the anchor. At (λ, J) = (0, 1), both frozen energies have

                                       |SY − 1| ≤ CT D−2α1

on a deeper half-plane. The argument of the fields is S(Y ), whose depth is comparable to D by the
original anchor. Integrate along vertical lines from infinity, where the original anchor makes S − Y

                                |S(Y ) − Y | ≤ CT D1−2α1 = o(D−1 ).                            (6.37)

Half-plane rigidity and the contradiction. Put v(Y ) = ℑ(Y − S(Y )). It is harmonic and satisfies
v ≥ −D, since S maps into the lower half-plane. We verify carefully that v ≥ 0. On a slab
ε < D < R, its top boundary is bounded below by −ε, and its bottom boundary is bounded
below by −ηR , where ηR = supx |S(x − iR) − (x − iR)| → 0. Compare on finite rectangles with the
harmonic function interpolating these two constants on the horizontal sides. The extra negative
contribution needed on the vertical sides is bounded by a constant depending on R times their
harmonic measure, because v ≥ −R in the slab. Their harmonic measure tends to zero when the
sides move to infinity. Thus
                                             R−D         D−ε
                                  v(Y ) ≥ −ε        − ηR       .
                                             R−ε         R−ε
First let ε ↓ 0, then R → ∞, to get v ≥ 0. Harnack’s inequality in the half-plane gives, for R ≥ 1,

                                        v(−iR) ≥ R−1 v(−i).

For example, this is the ordinary disk Harnack inequality after mapping −i to the origin, since
−iR has disk modulus (R − 1)/(R + 1). By (6.37) its left side is o(R−1 ). Hence v(−i) = 0, and the
strong minimum principle gives v ≡ 0. The holomorphic function S − Y is therefore real constant;
the anchor makes that constant zero. We have proved S(Y ) = Y .
    Finally, set J = 1 in (6.36). Deep down the field parameter is small, and the energy expansions
are
                              1     λ2                            1      λ2
                 EF (λ, 1) −    =−      + O(λ4 ),     EQ (λ, 1) −    = − + O(λ4 ).
                             12     480                           12     60
Each right side can vanish near zero only when λ2 = 0. Since T ̸= 0, the surviving pure field
vanishes on a deep open set, and hence identically. This contradicts its normalization at −i, proving
the Theorem.

7     Exponential profiles and exclusion of infinite growth
Throughout this Section we are in the nonisotropic, noncircular case of Theorem 5.4. Thus f = ϕ′
and q = f ′′ /f are holomorphic on DH = {s : |ℑs| < H}, where H may be infinite, and

                                            M (s) = max{|f (s)|, |q(s)|1/2 }.

The impact lift is denoted by s(y, t). Its normalization is s(y, 0) = y, and it is even in t. We shall
exclude a particular pair of rapidly varying field profiles. The point of retaining both profiles in the
statement is that the second can occur even when the normalized first field tends to zero.

Theorem 7.1 (Exponential-profile obstruction). There is no sequence of centers sn = xn +ihn ∈ DH ,
length scales ℓn > 0, and field scales mn > 0 with the following properties. Put bn = ℓn mn and

                      f (sn + ℓn S)                             q(sn + ℓn S)               y − sn
           Fn (S) =                 ,            Qn (S) =                    ,       Y =          ,     T = mn t.   (7.1)
                           mn                                       m2n                      ℓn

Assume that bn → ∞, that (DH − sn )/ℓn exhausts C, and that, locally uniformly on C, either

                 (Fn , Qn ) −→ (c0 e−iS , 0)               or   (Fn , Qn ) −→ (0, c20 e−2iS ),       c0 ̸= 0.       (7.2)

The following additional assumptions are imposed according to the value of H.
    (i) If H < ∞, then hn → H, ℓn → 0, and there are α0 ∈ (1/2, 1) and δb ∈ (0, H/2) such that, for
        every fixed L < ∞, there is a constant CL for which eventually
                                                 α0
                                            ℓn
                                        
                  M (s) ≤ CL mn                        ,        ℓn ≤ δ = hn − ℑs ≤ δb ,          |ℜs − xn | ≤ Lδ.   (7.3)
                                             δ
       The exponent is independent of L; the constant and the eventual sequence threshold may depend
       on L.
 (ii) If H = ∞, then hn → ∞, 0 < ℓn ≤ C, and (7.3) holds with a single constant for all horizontal
      positions and for ℓn ≤ δ ≤ hn . Moreover,

                                                  log(1 + hn /ℓn ) = o(log mn ).                                    (7.4)

    The proof has three parts. First we initialize the lift far below the center height, including at
complex parameters. Next we identify its unique limiting equation and a branch point of its solution.
Finally, we use real-parameter width continuation to make that branch point incompatible with a
single-valued impact lift.

7.1     Joint deep data
For a local continuation of the lift define
                                                            s(sn + ℓn Y, T /mn ) − sn
                                    Sn (Y, T ) =                                      .
                                                                       ℓn
A continuation is said to be reached vertically if its germs are obtained along the vertical segment
with the horizontal position and parameter fixed, starting in known lower data. Such germs agree
on overlapping neighborhoods: a finite chain of neighborhoods along the segment compares any two
sufficiently close vertical paths.


                                                                  70
Lemma 7.2 (Deep passages). Assume the hypotheses in Theorem 7.1, without assuming its
conclusion. Fix α ∈ (1/2, α0 ) and T0 < ∞. There are D0 and C, independent of the fixed aperture
A, with the following property. For every fixed A < ∞, and eventually in n, there are joint
holomorphic vertical passages on compact subsets of

                          D = −ℑY > D0 ,          |ℜY | < AD,         |T | < T0 ,                 (7.5)

and
                                    |Sn (Y, T ) − Y | ≤ C|T |2 D1−2α .                            (7.6)
These passages start in the actual lift domain and remain in the shape strip. Their eventual sequence
threshold may depend on A and on the compact subset.
Proof. We first make a uniformity observation. With D = δ/ℓn , weakening the exponent in (7.3)
gives
                                   Dα M/mn ≤ CL D−(α0 −α) .
For each fixed L, this is bounded by an absolute constant beyond a finite depth threshold depending
on L. On the remaining finite cone, (7.2) supplies the same bound eventually: the limiting value of
M/mn is |c0 |e−D , independent of the horizontal position. Consequently the weakened cone bound
can have a constant independent of L, provided its eventual sequence threshold is allowed to depend
on L. This permits the choice of D0 before a large aperture.
   Suppose first that H < ∞. Choose D0 ≥ 10, so the required input depth d/10 is at least ℓn .
Apply Theorem 6.3 and its reference-height formulation in Theorem 6.4 with reference height hn ,
rather than H, and with
                                                  2T0
                             d = ℓ n D0 ,    r=       ,      ε = C ′ T0 D0−α .
                                                  mn
The cone hypothesis is exactly rM ≤ ε(d/δ)α . Since r/d = 2T0 /(bn D0 ) → 0, the fixed ratio
allowance in that theorem can be chosen to be one. Its auxiliary bound satisfies

                                      B = 1 + (ε/r)dα ≤ Cmn ℓαn .

Here mn ℓαn → ∞, since bn → ∞ and ℓn → 0. The physical displacement estimate, divided by ℓn , is
therefore (7.6). Increasing D0 makes the required smallness conditions hold. The starting slab lies
in a fixed strict substrip of the original shape strip. The relative displacement is small compared
with the depth below hn ; in particular the image remains in DH . This use of an auxiliary reference
height needs no no-patch assertion at that height. The aperture assertion of Theorem 6.3, together
with the preceding weakening argument, gives the stated independence of D0 , C from A.
    We provide the initializer needed when H = ∞, because the starting height is then unbounded.
Retain the same d, r, and put B = Cmn ℓαn . All horizontal ranges may now be used. Condition (7.4)
implies
                                  r(hn /d)α −→ 0,       Bh−α
                                                          n −→ ∞.                               (7.7)
Thus the known data near ℑy = 0 have uniformly small parameters over the entire range that will
be used.
    Consider first |ℑt| ≥ γ|t|, with a fixed steep cutoff γ > 0. Use the upward increment t or −t, as
appropriate. The normalized chord recurrence and kernel estimates in Theorem 6.3 apply along the
segment from the real-row slab to depth δ ≥ d/2, for |t| ≤ 10r(δ/d)α . Indeed the fast fields are small
there after increasing D0 , while |t|/δ is bounded. Local Cauchy disks have radius a fixed fraction of
the depth. Any part of a thickening slightly below the real row is covered by real symmetry and the

                                                   71
comparable bound above that row. The recurrence estimates, with x denoting the current depth,
are
                    |J − 1| ≤ C|t|2 B 2 x−2α , |s − y| ≤ C|t|2 B 2 x1−2α .
To see the accumulation directly, the change of normalized step is O(|t|3 B 2 x−2α−1 ). Summation
over rows separated by a constant multiple of |t| gives the first estimate, and a second summation
gives the second because 2α > 1. Initial step and position errors of order |t|2 contribute at most
C|t|2 (1 + hn ) to the latter. This is absorbed, uniformly at all δ ≤ hn , because

                                       B 2 h1−2α
                                            n    /(1 + hn ) −→ ∞

by (7.7). The small relative-error induction consequently closes. Choosing the number of backward
steps locally constantly, with two starting entries inside the known slab, gives holomorphic germs.
Different choices agree by the exact recurrence and uniqueness of its nearby root.
    It remains to initialize all parameter directions at large depths. Fix a large constant K0 . At a
target depth hn /K0 ≤ δ ≤ hn , set Rt = r(δ/d)α . For |tc | < Rt consider

              t′ = tc + 3Rt ζ,     y = x + iσ + σg(ζ),       0 ≤ σ ≤ hn − δ,    g(0) = 0.           (7.8)

Choose a smaller fixed steep cutoff for this initializer. On the unit circle the nonsteep arcs for t′ are
uniformly short: the circle has radius 3Rt , its center has modulus at most Rt , and its intersections
with a sufficiently narrow horizontal sector are transverse. We choose a smooth real boundary
function of mean zero which equals −1 on these arcs and slightly larger collars, is nowhere below
−1, and is at most 1/(6K0 ). This is possible by making the arcs sufficiently short and balancing
the negative bumps by a small positive part. Approximate it, with its mean removed, by a real
trigonometric polynomial with error o(1/hn ). It is the boundary imaginary part of a holomorphic
polynomial g with g(0) = 0. In particular,

         1 + ℑg = o(1/hn ) on the nonsteep collars,        −o(1/hn ) ≤ 1 + ℑg ≤ 1 + 1/(3K0 )

on the whole circle. The smooth bumps can be chosen with uniform smooth bounds before
approximation, so g is uniformly bounded; its degree need not be fixed.
    On the nonsteep collars, (7.8) lands in the original fixed real-row neighborhood. On the other
arcs its depth is at least δ − hn /(3K0 ) ≥ 2δ/3, and the steep estimates just proved apply, or the
point is still in the base neighborhood. All parameter sizes tend to zero by (7.7). These boundary
germs glue as vertical continuations from below.
    Here and in subsequent disk fillings we use the following explicit holomorphic continuation
argument. Pull the boundary data back to a thin annulus about |ζ| = 1, times a complex
neighborhood of the center path. Their negative Laurent coefficients in ζ are holomorphic functions
of the center variables. Initially the full disks lie in known data, so all those coefficients vanish.
The identity theorem makes them vanish along the whole connected center path. The nonnegative
Laurent series fills each disk and gives joint holomorphic center germs. Applied to (s − y)/(t′ )2 ,
which is regular at t′ = 0 in the initial data, the maximum principle transfers the boundary estimate
to the disk center. Small complex changes of σ, tc are allowed by the strict margins above, so these
really are joint germs. This proves the all-direction estimate for hn /K0 ≤ δ ≤ hn , with constants
independent of hn .
    The dyadic disk construction in Theorem 6.3 now continues it from hn /K0 to d. Choose K0
first so large that its previous-band dips and starting slabs remain at positive heights. No factor
accumulates with the number of bands: the exponent 1 − 2α < 0 makes the estimates on the
deeper, previously known arcs strictly smaller, whereas the steep-arc estimates have an independent

                                                   72
fixed constant. Fix both steep cutoffs and K0 , and only then increase D0 to meet their smallness
requirements. After scaling by ℓn , the result is (7.6). In the infinite-width case the shape constraint
is automatic. This completes both cases.

Lemma 7.3 (Deep model and its uniqueness). On the deep domains of Theorem 7.2, the passages
converge locally uniformly, with derivatives, to a unique holomorphic model S∗ (Y, T ). With λ =

                                  EF (λ, J) = 1/12      or   EQ (λ, J) = 1/12,                            (7.10)

Proof. The deep estimates and Cauchy’s inequalities give locally normal families and the asserted

division in Theorem 6.1, rather than division of two limiting zeros.
    The exact recurrence takes the form

                      pn,J (S, J) − an pn,S (S, J) = pn,J (S− , J− ),      S = S− + an J− .               (7.11)

It holds on the passages by analytic continuation. To fix its root choice, first take T sufficiently
small, for each fixed n, so that all physical points in question lie in known strict substrips with the
glancing branch. Continue in the deep T -disk. Its shifted arguments remain in the domain with
margin, and Cauchy estimates keep Jn near one. The fast fields are uniformly small after increasing
D0 , so the relevant kernels and roots remain nondegenerate throughout that continuation.

its sum of absolute coefficients by the real solution v(ρ) < vc . It converges uniformly on the closed
circle and the regular differential equation (7.16) continues it through every point of that circle, a
contradiction. At radius R its positive sum is vc . At phases other than zero and π, the modulus is
strictly smaller than vc : equality in the triangle inequality for the strictly positive linear and cubic
terms would require e2iθ = 1. The differential equation therefore gives continuation through those
phases.

7.4   Tracking and passage through regular corridors
Lemma 7.5 (Tracking a regular model corridor). Let a compact corridor of a continued branch of
the model in Theorem 7.4 have nonzero kernels and twist, and let T range over a compact set avoiding
zero. Suppose two neighboring lift entries are initialized in a joint patch where they converge to that
model, with convergence of their first divided difference. Along segments of bounded length made of
increments an = T /bn , the normalized chord recurrence continues these entries holomorphically and
tracks the model with error o(1). The same holds for increments −T /bn . The statement is uniform
on compact thickenings of such segments and their initial patches.


                                                       76
Proof. Cover the compact model states by finitely many analytic action charts with kernels and
pJJ bounded away from zero. The analytic action convergence in Theorem 6.2 applies on these
charts, following the branches initialized in the known data. Given the old state (S− , J− ), set
S = S− + an J− . Equation (7.11) has a unique nearby root for J by the implicit function theorem.
Evaluating its left side at J = J− , and subtracting before taking the limit, gives

                                           pS − J− pSJ
                             J − J− = an               (S− , J− ) + o(|an |).
                                               pJJ
The error is uniform with state derivatives on the compact charts. Indeed the corresponding
expression with pn has a Taylor remainder O(|an |2 ), and its coefficient converges with derivatives to
the displayed one. The state map consequently is
                                                       pS − J− pSJ
                                                                              
                 (S− , J− ) 7−→ (S− , J− ) + an J− ,               (S− , J− ) + o(|an |),        (7.18)
                                                           pJJ

and has local Lipschitz bound 1 + C|an |.
    The model solves the ordinary differential equation in (7.18): its second component follows
by expanding dY pJ = pS . In O(1/|an |) steps, the initial error and the local o(|an |) errors sum,
with a bounded stability factor, to o(1). This proves tracking and keeps the continued states in
the chosen regular charts. The shape arguments remain in the exhausting shape domains. The
implicit roots are holomorphic also in the initial point and parameter; the same estimates on slightly
larger compact charts give uniform convergence on thickenings. For the negative increment use the
fast fields with −T . The frozen actions are even in λ, so their limit is unchanged, and the same
calculation applies.
    At an initialization obtained by previous regular passage, the required exact recurrence and
its branch choice persist by analytic identity from the deep data. Cauchy’s integral formula
gives convergence of the first divided differences whenever the two-entry functions converge on
neighborhoods. Thus the construction can be reinitialized in every regular patch it reaches.

    One consequence will be used repeatedly. If |ℑT | ≥ η > 0, choose the upward increment
an = ±T /bn . A target can be reached from an initialized lower slab whenever its backward slanted
segment remains in one regular corridor. Choose the number of backward steps locally constantly so
that two entries lie well inside the slab, and then apply Theorem 7.5. The germs from different such
choices agree: within the initial slab their overlapping entries obey the exact recurrence, and every
subsequent nearby root is unique. Doing this at intermediate targets gives the germs along the
vertical segment from below. There is room for small changes in both the target and the parameter,
so the passage is joint and converges on compact neighborhoods. Its horizontal drift is bounded
by a constant times the vertical distance divided by η. Thus any fixed positive η, however small,
requires only a fixed finite horizontal range before n tends to infinity.

Proof of Theorem 7.1. Let
                                    T∗ = |c0 |−1 ,        r0 = T∗ /32,
so that T∗ is real and |T∗ c0 | = 1. Take the parameter bound in Theorem 7.2 larger than all the
parameter disks below. We first reach, vertically and with joint convergence, the inner regular region

                               |T c0 e−iY | ≤ Re−ν ,       |T − T∗ | ≤ 2r0 ,                     (7.19)

for any fixed ν > 0 and every needed compact horizontal range.


                                                     77
   Fix a center Tc in this parameter disk and use

                  T ′ = Tc + 8r0 ζ,     Y ′ = Yc − i log(T ′ /Tc ) + g(ζ),       g(0) = 0.         (7.20)

The parameter disk is bounded away from zero, so this logarithm is single-valued. Its sign is
important:
                                                  ′
                               T ′ e−i[Yc −i log(T /Tc )] = Tc e−iYc .
Choose a small fixed η > 0. On the unit circle require the boundary imaginary part of g to be a
sufficiently large negative constant on the arcs |ℑT ′ | < η and slightly larger collars, and to be at
most ν/2 everywhere. It must also have mean zero. Such a choice is possible: the bad arcs have
arbitrarily small total length as η ↓ 0, uniformly in the allowed centers, by transversality of the
parameter circles. Use smooth negative bumps on those arcs, balance their mean by a positive
part smaller than ν/2, then approximate by a trigonometric polynomial with strict margins. The
corresponding holomorphic polynomial can be normalized by g(0) = 0.
    On the bad arcs the adjusted point lies in deep initialized data. On the other arcs, use the
upward increment ±T ′ /bn to track from a deep slab. Every intermediate point of its backward
slanted segment has smaller |T ′ c0 e−iY | than the target, so the whole segment lies in the inner regular
model region. The horizontal range needed for these paths, including the shift by g, can be large,
but it is fixed after η is chosen. The depth D0 in Theorem 7.2 was independent of that aperture,
so the initialization remains available. The boundary germs agree as vertical continuations and
converge to the model on compact neighborhoods.
    Raise ℑYc from a deep initial position to the desired target in (7.19). By the maximum principle
for ℑg, the full model disks in (7.20) stay within radius Re−ν/2 . Initially the full disks lie in known
data. The Laurent-coefficient argument used in the proof of Theorem 7.2 fills the disks as the
center rises, giving joint center germs. Apply the maximum principle to the difference between
the filled lift and the analytic model. Boundary convergence gives convergence at the centers, and
on neighborhoods by retaining the strict margins. This proves (7.19). The exact chord identities,
including neighboring arguments, continue through these passages for all sufficiently large n.
    We next reach two regular patches just beyond the model circle, near the phases

                        w = Reu+iθ    and    w = Reu−iθ ,        u ≤ ν,      T = T∗ .              (7.21)

First fix a sufficiently small θ > 0; then choose ν > 0 sufficiently small in terms of θ. Use parameter
circles of radius r0 , so that their initial points remain in the parameter range already reached, and
use the same logarithmic adjustment as in (7.20). This time choose a polynomial with

                  ℑg ≤ −4ν on |ℑT ′ | < η1 and collars,          ℑg ≤ ν,        |g| ≤ C1 ν.

The cutoff η1 > 0 and constant C1 can be fixed independently of ν: first choose the sufficiently
short bad arcs, construct one mean-zero adjustment, and multiply it by ν.
   Raise the centers from u = −Cν, with C a sufficiently large fixed constant, to u = ν. The
nonsteep arcs remain in the known inner region. On the steep arcs, tracking starts just below
u = −ν, and the additional vertical distance is O(ν). The horizontal drift is therefore O(ν/η1 ).
Choose ν so small that this drift and the real displacement of g stay in the chosen regular phase
corridor about each sign in (7.21). The full model disks stay in that corridor as well. Disk filling
and maximum modulus, exactly as above, give vertical joint passages and compact convergence to
the two radial model continuations.
   At the real physical parameter t = T∗ /mn , one of these passages reaches the physical height

                                          hn + ℓn (log R + ν).

                                                    78
It remains in the shape strip: this was proved in the deep part and follows on the remaining
compact path from model convergence and the exhaustion of scaled shape domains. Consequently
it gives joint meromorphic germs for X = Γ(s), attached to the primary branch from below. If
the real-parameter joint width ended at an earlier height, the passage would give a prohibited
joint patch at its first wall, contrary to Theorem 4.9. The same argument excludes equality at the
endpoint, because the passage has a neighborhood there. Thus, for all sufficiently large n,

                                  hX (T∗ /mn ) > hn + ℓn (log R + ν).                             (7.22)

This is the one place where a local passage is promoted to an entire horizontal band, and the
parameter here is real. By Theorem 5.4, Sn (Y, T∗ ) is a single-valued holomorphic function on a
neighborhood of the full row ℑY = log R + ν/2. The two vertical passages agree with this actual
lift, by analytic identity from their lower starting segments.
     Follow this row from phase +θ to phase −θ, increasing ℜY , with real steps an = T∗ /bn > 0.
Initialize in the first of the patches (7.21). The continued model follows the outside of w = R. At its
endpoint it differs from the independent radial continuation to the other patch: close the comparison
path through the inner disk, and the resulting loop winds once around R, while avoiding −R. This
is the nontrivial continuation proved in Theorem 7.4. All states of the two paths and their small
thickenings are regular if θ, ν have been chosen small enough.
     For clarity, we verify why recurrence tracking on this horizontal path must agree with the actual
single-valued lift. Take a small fixed complex ball B∗ in the initial patch, and run the tracking
construction for each initial offset in B∗ . Its successive output germs live on B∗ + jan . For an
offset Y and the offset Y + an , their first two common entries coincide by the actual data and the
exact recurrence in the initial patch; take B∗ with room for two extra steps. Uniqueness of every
subsequent nearby root makes the chains coincide on their common entries. Hence the germs on
adjacent translated balls agree on overlap. They glue, and by the identity theorem agree throughout
with the actual holomorphic function on the band supplied by (7.22). Tracking gives convergence to
the horizontally continued model on these balls.
     Choose a number of steps whose endpoint tends to a point in the second regular patch; the
remaining offset tends to zero. Compact convergence on neighborhoods makes this harmless. At
that point the same actual function would converge both to the horizontally continued model and
to the distinct radial model value. This contradiction proves the Theorem.

7.5   A log-deficit estimate
We next produce an exponential profile from either an entire nonconstant first field or growth of
infinite order at a finite wall. The quotient condition q = f ′′ /f being holomorphic is used to control
the second field from a logarithmic bound on the first.

Lemma 7.6 (Log-deficit estimate). There is an absolute constant C such that the following holds.
Let f be holomorphic and not identically zero on the unit disk, suppose log |f | ≤ K, and suppose
f ′′ /f extends holomorphically through every zero. Then
                                                      Z                              !
                    sup |f ′′ (z)/f (z)|1/2 ≤ C 1 +            (K − log |f (z)|) dA(z) .          (7.23)
                   |z|<1/4                             |z|<1


On a disk of radius ∆, the corresponding left side is ∆ sup |q|1/2 and the integral on the right is
replaced by the disk average of the log deficit.



                                                  79
                                                 R
Proof. Put U = K − log |f | ≥ 0 and A = 1 + D U . We may assume that this integral is finite. The
distribution −∆U is 2π times the zero-counting measure, including multiplicities. Testing it against
a nonnegative smooth cutoff equal to one on |z| ≤ 0.8 and supported in the unit disk bounds the
number of those zeros by CA. If they are zj , listed with multiplicity, then
                                                     X
                                        V =U+               log |z − zj |
                                                       j

is harmonic on |z| < 0.8. On a slightly smaller disk it has L1 norm O(A), since each logarithm
has uniformly bounded L1 norm there. Interior harmonic estimates bound its first and second
derivatives by CA, with a further fixed margin.
    Equivalently, divide f by its listed zero factors. The zero-free quotient has a holomorphic
logarithm in the disk, whose first two derivatives are controlled by the derivatives of V . Off the
zeros, differentiating the logarithm of f gives

                                 |f ′′ /f |1/2 ≤ CA + C             |z − zj |−1
                                                               X

                                                                j

on a fixed smaller disk. Here the second derivative of the logarithm contributes square roots
of sums of |z − zj |−2 , bounded by the displayed sum. Each summand is locally integrable in
two real dimensions with a uniform integral bound, and there are at most CA of them. The
resulting L1 bound for |q|1/2 , followed by submean for that subharmonic function, proves (7.23). Its
subharmonicity across the zeros uses the assumed holomorphic extension of q. Rescale the variable
to obtain the final assertion.

   We record the periodic form that will also be useful below. The horizontal mean
                                                Z 2π
                                            1
                                  L(h) =               log |f (x + ih)| dx
                                           2π    0

is convex in height by subharmonicity, and even by real symmetry. Thus L(h) ≥ L(0) > −∞;
the real-row value is finite since f > 0 there. If K bounds log |f | at all heights of disks of radius
∆, averaging their average deficits over the horizontal centers gives O(1 + K+ ), with constants
depending only on the fixed real-row data. For each individual center positivity and periodicity give
the bound
                       1 + disk-average(K − log |f |) ≤ C(1 + ∆−1 )(1 + K+ ).                    (7.24)
Indeed, enclose the disk in a rectangle of width and height 2∆. At each height an interval of
that width is covered by O(1 + ∆) whole periods; its integral of the nonnegative deficit is at most
C(1 + ∆)(1 + K+ ). Integration in height and division by the disk area gives (7.24). In particular,
on a smaller disk,
                               |q|1/2 ≤ C∆−1 (1 + ∆−1 )(1 + K+ ).                             (7.25)

7.6   Selecting a profile at entire or infinite-order growth
Theorem 7.7 (Exclusion of entire and infinite-order growth). In the nonisotropic case, H = ∞
implies that the table is a circle. If H < ∞, then

                                       log maxx M (x + i(H − e−k ))
                             lim sup                                < ∞.
                              k→∞                   k



                                                       80
Proof. We may exclude constant f , which gives a circle. Suppose first that H < ∞ and the displayed
limsup is infinite. The same is true with M replaced by |f |. Otherwise log max |f | = O(k) on those
rows; disks with radius comparable to e−k , and with a fixed extra depth margin, have a global
log upper bound O(k). Formula (7.25) then gives a finite power bound in inverse depth for |q|1/2 ,
contradicting the assumed infinite order of M .
    Treat this case and H = ∞ together. Put

                                      ρ(h) = log max |f (x + ih)|.
                                                   x

The function ρ is convex in height by the three-lines theorem, and even by symmetry. In the finite-
width case just identified it tends to infinity and has infinite upper order relative to log(1/(H − h)).
In the entire case a nonconstant real-symmetric periodic function has a growing Fourier mode: if a
nonconstant coefficient is nonzero, its conjugate coefficient is also nonzero, and one grows as h → ∞.
Hence ρ(h) ≥ ch − C for some c > 0. In either case ρ → ∞, and it is eventually strictly increasing
with slope bounded below by a positive constant.
    Let h(R) denote its inverse at large values, let N (R) be its right slope there, and put µ(R) =
log N (R). The function µ is nondecreasing and bounded below. The inverse is locally absolutely
continuous and
                                 h′ (R) = e−µ(R) for almost every R.
We claim that
                                          lim inf µ(R)/R = 0.                                      (7.26)
                                          R→∞

If µ(R) ≥ cR eventually for some c > 0, inverse integration would give a finite limiting height and
H − h(R) ≤ Ce−cR . This is impossible if H = ∞. For finite H it implies R/ log(1/(H − h(R))) ≤
1/c + o(1), contradicting infinite upper order. Since µ is bounded below, the liminf cannot be
negative. This proves (7.26).
    Choose R → ∞ along values with µ(R) = o(R), and fix a lower base value Rb . The positive
measure dµ on [Rb , R] has mass o(R). The weak maximal-interval inequality gives a number εR → 0
and a continuity point v ∈ [R/3, 2R/3] such that

                             |µ(z) − µ(v)| ≤ εR |z − v|     (Rb ≤ z ≤ R).                          (7.27)

Here is a direct measure justification of the choice. The set of points belonging to an interval on
which the mass divided by its length exceeds εR has length at most a constant times the total mass
   p by εR , by the disjoint-interval covering argument. Choose, for example, a constant multiple
divided
of (1 + µ(R) − µ(Rb ))/R for εR . The bad set has length o(R), so cannot cover [R/3, 2R/3].
Increasing the constant accounts for intervals with one endpoint at the selected point. Atoms and
discontinuity points may be discarded. This gives both one-sided secant bounds in (7.27).
    Set
                           hn = h(v),       ℓn = N (v)−1 ,    mn = ev .                       (7.28)
For bounded u, integrate the inverse derivative using (7.27), then invert the result. It gives, uniformly
on each fixed bounded real range,

                                     ρ(hn + ℓn u) = v + u + o(1).                                  (7.29)

These heights stay in the shape strip: bounded intervals of values about v lie in [Rb , R] with distances
tending to infinity from the endpoints, and the inverse integration applies there. In particular the
upper-wall distance in units of ℓn tends to infinity.


                                                   81
   The slope lower bound, and µ(v) ≤ µ(R) = o(R), also give
   0 < ℓn ≤ C,       hn ≤ Cv,            | log ℓn | = o(v),           bn = ℓn mn = exp(v − o(v)) −→ ∞.       (7.30)
The centers satisfy hn → H, with the infinite value allowed. For finite H, convex divergence at that
height forces the slopes to infinity, so ℓn → 0. For infinite H, hn → ∞ and ℓn ≤ C. Thus the lower
wall also escapes in scaled coordinates, and the shape domains exhaust C.
    Choose xn realizing the row maximum at hn . By (7.29), the entire subsequential limits of
                                        Fn (S) = m−1

satisfy |F (S)| ≤ eℑS and |F (0)| = 1. The maximum principle applied to eiS F (S) therefore gives
                                            F (S) = c0 e−iS ,          |c0 | = 1.                            (7.31)
Normal-family extraction, including the phase at zero, supplies such locally uniform convergence.
    The normalized second field tends to zero. In fact, for each fixed C1 , use disks of radius a
fixed small multiple of ℓn throughout |ℑs| ≤ hn + C1 ℓn , with an extra margin of a few such radii.
Symmetry and (7.29) give the global log bound K = v + OC1 (1) on their heights. Equations

Consequently Qn = q/m2n → 0 on every scaled compact set.
   We finish by verifying the coarser bounds, including their full range to a fixed physical depth.
For Rb ≤ v ′ ≤ v, inverse integration and (7.27) give
               h(v) − h(v ′ )

Fix any α0 ∈ (1/2, 1). At height h(v ′ ), multiply the row bound |f |/mn ≤ e−(v−v ) by the α0 -power

                                  (v − v ′ )α0 exp −(1 − α0 εR )(v − v ′ ) ,

which is bounded uniformly. Hence the required power bound for |f |/mn holds globally in the

                                      log(1 + hn /ℓn ) = o(v)                           (7.34)

uniformly in horizontal position. In the finite-width case retain any fixed smaller range δ ≤ δb < H/2.
In the entire case (7.34) is exactly the additional condition (7.4).
    All hypotheses of Theorem 7.1 now hold, with the first profile in (7.2), by (7.31) and (7.32). Its
contradiction proves both assertions.

    We can now assemble the growth alternatives. In the nonisotropic, noncircular case, Theorem 7.7
makes H finite and the upper growth order finite. An upper order less than one would give a
uniform bound by a power of inverse depth with exponent less than one, contrary to Theorem 6.6.
A finite upper order greater than one is excluded by Theorem 6.8. Thus the only remaining case has
a finite shape wall and upper growth order exactly one. This is the critical case considered next.

that finite periodic strip, followed by η ↓ 0, proves (8.6).

8.1   Envelopes, tiles, and the one-sided estimate
Definition 8.1. An envelope is a function N : [k0 , ∞) → [1, ∞) for which there are fixed constants
CN ≥ 1, 0 ≤ β < 1/2, and a > 0 such that
                                                  (
                                   N (j)           eβ(k−j) ,    k0 ≤ j ≤ k,
                                         ≤ CN                                                       (8.7)
                                   N (k)           ea(j−k) ,    j ≥ k.

   For the estimates below we impose the first-moment bound

                                     E(dk M ) ≤ C1 N (k),         k ≥ k0 .                          (8.8)

When using a bootstrap, we also assume that supk≥k0 µ(k)/N (k) < ∞; no bound on this finite
number is assumed. All conclusions below are uniform in it. In particular, an envelope that
eventually grows at any fixed positive exponential rate has this finiteness property by (8.2).
    The constants in the ensuing estimates may depend on the table, k0 , CN , C1 , β, a, and on specified
fixed tile enlargements. They do not depend on k, on the finite bootstrap ratio, or on the particular
envelope in a family with these constants fixed.
    For A ≥ 1, a tile about (k, x) will mean a set contained in

                 TA (k, x) = {s : e−A dk ≤ H − ℑs ≤ eA dk , |ℜs − x| ≤ A(H − ℑs)}.                  (8.9)

Horizontal distance is interpreted on a real lift and then periodically. We intersect tiles with the
upper half of the shape strip. If a tile reaches a fixed lower region, we use the analytic bounds there.
In particular, all such fixed lower contributions are harmless because N ≥ 1.
    We record the elementary averaging fact used repeatedly. For every p > 0, the function M p is
subharmonic: it is the maximum of |f |p and |q|p/2 . Covering a fixed tile by a bounded number of
disks of radius comparable to dk , and using the submean inequality on slightly larger disks, bounds
its supremum by Cd−2  k times the integral on a fixed enlargement. Consequently,

                           E     sup    (dk M (s))p ≤ CA,p       sup      E(dj M )p ,             (8.10)
                               s∈TA (k,x)                      |j−k|≤CA

with the fixed lower-region convention just specified. One may replace the supremum on the right
by a bounded average of neighboring rows. To obtain the displayed form, integrate the disk bound
in x; each point of the enlargement is counted for a horizontal length O(dk ), and its vertical range
has length O(dk ).
    Choose β < β1 < 1/2, a sufficiently large fixed aperture L, a sufficiently small fixed c0 > 0, and
a fixed δb ∈ (0, H/2). We take δb sufficiently small that the fixed enlargements of cone tiles used
below, in particular in Theorem 11.2, have upper-boundary depth at most e−k0 . This is possible
after the fixed cone and tile apertures have been chosen. Define

8.3   A weighted version with an ancestor row
We will need to concentrate horizontal centers near a selected ancestor, while retaining estimates
at much finer depths. For 0 < da ≪ 1, let Kda be a normalized periodic probability kernel whose
density, relative to dx/(2π), is uniformly comparable to
                                                       da
                                                                .                                (8.29)
                                                   da + |x|2per
                                                    2


The normalization factor in this comparison is bounded above and below independently of da . Given
any probability ω on the circle, put ν = Kda ∗ ω. The following elementary properties will be used.
    First, for every fixed A, the density of ν changes by at most a fixed factor under translations of
length at most Ada . For the kernel this follows from the triangle inequality and

                           d2a + |u + h|2per ≤ CA (d2a + |u|2per )   (|h| ≤ Ada ),

and convolution preserves the comparison. Second, if dj < da /2, the unnormalized upper-boundary
harmonic-measure law from the ancestor point x+i(H −da ) to the row at depth dj , with real bottom,

                                                        88
is uniformly comparable to the translated kernel Kda . Indeed in (8.23) the distance from the upper
row lies between da /2 and da . On a fundamental horizontal interval the denominator is comparable
to d2a + |u|2 , with constants depending only on H; periodization contributes a comparable bounded
tail. Mixing over ω gives an unnormalized law comparable to ν.

Proof. It suffices first to bound row norms. Let C∗ be their supremum ratio to N on the fine levels,
enlarged to include the fixed coarse constants. This number is finite. For example, the density of ν
is bounded for each fixed ancestor, so finiteness follows from the unweighted finite-ratio hypothesis.
This preliminary bound may depend on da ; none of the estimates below will depend on it.
    Tile averaging on levels whose depths are at most a fixed multiple of da is valid for ν, by its
translation-comparison property. In the logarithmic summation defining b, use these fine tile bounds
on that part of the cone, and use (8.30) on the coarser part. This gives

                                            ∥b(·, j)∥L2 (ν) ≤ C(1 + C∗ ).                          (8.33)

Fixed ranges immediately adjacent to the coarse cutoff are already covered by the assumed fixed
tile enlargements at levels at most ka + 3. In particular, choosing larger fixed interior-estimate
neighborhoods does not create a gap at the cutoff.
    For dj < da /2, let κj be the mixed unnormalized harmonic-measure law from ω to the row at
depth dj . Since P = 0 on the real bottom,
                      Z                                            Z
                          P (x + i(H − dj )) dκj (x) =                 P (x + i(H − da )) dω(x).

The laws κj and ν are comparable. Moreover the absolute value of the right side, in fine-row
normalization, is at most
                             dj N (ka )2
                           C             ≤ Ce−(1−2β)(j−ka ) ≤ C.                       (8.34)
                             da N (j)2
The negative-part estimate (8.15), integrated using (8.33), bounds the normalized negative integral
by C(1 + C∗m ). Taking the signed integral under κj first, and then using comparison of the two
positive measures, therefore gives
                                   dj |P (x + i(H − dj ))|
                               Z
                                                           dν(x) ≤ C(1 + C∗m ).
                                            N (j)2

                                                              89
The strict inequality 2β < 1 gives uniform decay in (8.34).
    Weighted interior harmonic estimates now follow by the same local averaging argument, since
the required horizontal translations are of size O(dj ). Combining them with Theorem 8.3 gives
C∗2 ≤ C(1 + C∗m ), away from the already controlled fixed cutoff range. This bounds C∗ uniformly.
One more application of weighted tile averaging proves (8.32).

8.4    A vanishing profile at one center is impossible
The next result is deliberately stated for individual centers. A single quiet cone forces a global
real-parameter width estimate, and the latter can then be tested against unweighted horizontal
moments.

Compact holomorphic convergence bounds its left side. Dividing by Bn2 and passing to the limit
gives F Q = 0. On the connected lower half-plane, a nonzero analytic factor then forces the other
factor to vanish identically.

8.5   The sequence to be excluded and the angle-budget reduction
We specify the input to the next Section before deriving the consequences of its exclusion. For a
probability νn on horizontal centers, convergence in probability uniformly on a compact set means
that the probability of the supremum exceeding each fixed positive threshold tends to zero.
Definition 8.7. A prohibited sequence consists of the following data in the critical setting of this
Section:
  (i) Levels kn → ∞, amplitudes Bn = µ(kn ) → ∞, and numbers εn ↓ 0 such that

                                     µ(j) ≤ Bn eεn |j−kn |      (j ≥ k0 ).                     (8.40)

 (ii) Ancestors ka,n = kn − sn , with both ka,n → ∞ and sn → ∞, and probabilities ωn and
      νn = Kda,n ∗ ωn , where da,n = e−ka,n and the kernels obey (8.29) with comparison constants
      independent of n.

                                                  91
(iii) For every fixed 0 < β < 1/2 and every fixed tile enlargement A, there is a constant Cβ,A such
      that, eventually in n, simultaneously for all k0 ≤ j ≤ ka,n + 3,


                                         sup      dj M (s)              ≤ Cβ,A Bn eβ(kn −j) .          (8.41)
                                     s∈TA (j,x)              L2 (νn )

     For every such β there is also a constant Cβ such that, eventually,

                                                                                Bn2 e2β(kn −ka,n )
                           Z

     The constants and the threshold for “eventually” may depend on the indicated fixed choices,
     but not on j or n.

                                         2

   For fixed β, a > 0, with β < 1/2, primary normality controls the unweighted moments by an
envelope of the form                                               
                           Nn (j) = Bn exp β(kn − j)+ + a(j − kn )+ .
Thus Theorem 8.5 applies to this envelope using (8.41) and (8.42). This observation uses none of
the conditional conclusions below. It is the reason the ancestors are included in the definition.
   We will also use an elementary way to propagate a logarithmic deficit. If U ≥ 0 is superharmonic
and two small disks have comparable radii, nearby centers, and a sufficiently enlarged common disk
contained in its domain, then
                                        avD2 U ≤ C avD1 U.                                     (8.44)
Indeed, for each point of D1 , a disk centered there and of a fixed multiple of the common radius
contains D2 and remains inside the domain. Positivity and the supermean inequality bound the
average on D2 by a constant times the value at that point; integration over D1 proves the claim.
Starting with a disk centered at a point where U is finite, the first average is at most that value. A
chain of m such steps therefore costs at most C m . In the upper strip, one can connect points with
logarithmic depth separation s, and with a horizontal displacement bounded by a fixed multiple
of the larger depth, by O(1 + s) disks of radii small fixed multiples of their depths. All enlarged
disks can be kept in a fixed slightly wider range of logarithmic depths. This argument applies to
K − log |f |, including its logarithmic poles at zeros; it uses disk averages, not a pointwise Harnack
inequality for superharmonic functions.

9    The critical moment law and its first-field model
We now establish the missing input to the critical estimates: the exclusion of the sequences in
Theorem 8.7. The exclusion has two parts. A sufficiently steep part of the moment law produces
one of the exponential profiles already ruled out by Theorem 7.1. Otherwise, after another change
of scale, the first field has a nonzero limit. Its limiting impact equation forces both an upper growth
estimate and large values near every boundary location. These two estimates are incompatible with
the fact that the quotient of its second derivative by the field is holomorphic.


                                                         96
Theorem 9.1 (Exclusion of the critical sequence). There is no sequence satisfying Theorem 8.7.

   We prove the theorem below. Until that proof is completed, neither the angle budget nor the
polynomial bounds conditional on this theorem will be used.

9.1   The limiting moment law
Suppose, for contradiction, that a prohibited sequence is given. Write its primary level, depth, and
normalization as

                     k = kn ,      d = dn = e−kn ,       B = Bn = µ(kn ) −→ ∞.

Thus, for some εn → 0,
                                    µ(l) ≤ Beεn |l−k|      (l ≥ k0 ).                            (9.1)
Expectation without a subscript in a horizontal moment means normalized Lebesgue measure on
one period. At physical depth dD, set

                                      d                1/2   µ(k − log D)
                           mn (D) =       E |f |2 + |q|       =              .                   (9.2)
                                      B                             BD
Each fixed compact subinterval of 0 < D < ∞ is contained in the domain of this definition for all
sufficiently large n.

Lemma 9.2 (Compactness of the moment law). After passage to a subsequence, mn converges
locally uniformly on (0, ∞) to a positive function m. It satisfies

               m(1) = 1,        m(D) ≤ D−1 ,         log m is convex as a function of D.         (9.3)

Proof. For a periodic holomorphic function, its horizontal Lp norm, p ≥ 1, is log-convex in the height.
One way to see this is to regard horizontal translates of the function as a Banach-space-valued
analytic function and apply the three-lines theorem to bounded linear functionals. Equivalently,
apply the scalar three-lines theorem followed by the dual description of the Lp norm. We use this for
the L2 norm of f and the L1 norm of q. If A and C are nonnegative log-convex functions, then their
sum is log-convex: the required interpolation inequality follows from Holder’s inequality applied to
A1−θ   θ
  0 A1 + C0
            1−θ θ
                C1 . Consequently log mn is convex. A field which is identically zero can simply be
omitted from this argument.
    The normalization gives mn (1) = 1, and (9.1) gives

                                    mn (D) ≤ D−1 exp(εn | log D|).

Thus log mn is uniformly bounded above on every compact subinterval. Convexity and its value zero
at 1 give a lower bound there as well. For example, if D < 1 < D+ , use the convexity inequality at 1,
viewed as a convex combination of D and D+ , and the upper bound at D+ . For D > 1, use a point
D− < 1. Bounds on a slightly larger compact interval bound the slopes of a convex function on the
smaller one. Arzela–Ascoli and a diagonal subsequence therefore give a finite locally uniform limit
for log mn . Exponentiating proves positivity, and all the properties in (9.3) pass to the limit.

   Put
                                         ρ(x) = − log m(ex ).                                    (9.4)




                                                   97
This use of ρ concerns only the moment law. It is unrelated to the curvature radius of the original
billiard. The function ρ is increasing and locally Lipschitz, and

                                       ρ(0) = 0,          ρ(x) ≥ x.

Indeed, the convex function log m(D) tends to −∞ as D → ∞, so each of its one-sided derivatives
is strictly negative. With the left derivative used at exceptional points, let p(x) = ρ′− (x) > 0.
Monotonicity of the one-sided derivative of log m gives

                                     p(v) ≤ ev−u p(u)        (v ≥ u).                              (9.5)

Lemma 9.3 (Contact dichotomy). Fix c ∈ (1/2, 1), and define
                                                                      
                                     ρc (x) = cx + inf ρ(v) − cv .                                 (9.6)
                                                     v≥x

A point is called a contact if ρ(x) = ρc (x). Exactly one of the following alternatives is needed.
  (i) There are contacts tending to +∞ at which p is unbounded. In that case there exist xj → ∞,
      Pj = p(xj ) → ∞, and Dj = exj such that

                                          m(Dj (1 − u/Pj ))
                                                            −→ eu                                  (9.7)
                                              m(Dj )

     uniformly on bounded real u-intervals. For every fixed α0 ∈ (1/2, c), these choices can also
     satisfy
                           m(Dj (1 + v/Pj ))
                                             ≤ C(1 + v)−α0       (v ≥ 1),                   (9.8)
                                 m(Dj )
     with C independent of j.
 (ii) The slopes at all sufficiently large contacts are bounded. There exist xj → ∞, a locally Lipschitz
      function ρ∗ with ρ∗ (0) = 0, and a finite λ ≥ 1 such that

                                        ρ(xj + u) − ρ(xj ) −→ ρ∗ (u)                               (9.9)

     locally uniformly, and

                              ρ∗ (u) = λu + o(|u|)         (u → +∞ or u → −∞).                    (9.10)

     There are constants C, P ′ > 0, independent of j, for which

                        ρ(v) ≥ ρ(xj ) + c(v − xj ) − C                     (v ≥ xj ),             (9.11)
                                           ′
                        ρ(v) ≥ ρ(xj ) − P (xj − v) − C                     (v ≤ xj ).             (9.12)

     Moreover ρ(xj ) = O(1 + xj ).
Proof. Since ρ(v) − cv ≥ (1 − c)v, the infimum in (9.6) is attained. The same inequality gives

                                          x ≤ ρc (x) ≤ ρ(x).

The running infimum is locally Lipschitz: when its lower endpoint is moved, its value can increase
only because a minimizer lay in the swept interval, where the local Lipschitz bound for ρ − cx
applies. On an open interval containing no contact the minimizer remains to the right, so ρc is
affine of slope c. Everywhere, ρc (v) − ρc (u) ≥ c(v − u) for v ≥ u.

                                                     98
   First suppose there are contacts zj → ∞ with p(zj ) → ∞. By (9.5),

                                  p(x) ≥ e−1 p(zj )        (zj − 1 ≤ x ≤ zj ).

For large j, ρ(x) − cx is strictly increasing on this interval. Each point in it is itself a contact: values
between that point and zj are larger, and values beyond zj are at least the value at zj because zj is
a contact.
    Set g = p−1/2 on this interval. Inequality (9.5) says that ex/2 g(x) is nondecreasing. If σj is the
supremum of g on the interval, then σj → 0, and the total variation of g there is O(σj ). To choose
a point where this small total variation is locally well distributed, let νj be its variation measure.
The elementary weak maximal inequality for intervals gives
                            (                                       )
                                        νj ((x − h, x + h)) √                 √
                                x : sup                    > σj           = O( σj ).
                                    h>0         2h

Here intervals are restricted to the original unit interval. This inequality follows by selecting disjoint
bad intervals and covering their union by their fivefold enlargements. Choose xj outside this bad
set in the middle third of the unit interval. Then, whenever |h| is smaller than its distance to the
endpoints,
                                                             √
                                     |g(xj + h) − g(xj )| ≤ 2 σj |h|.
                         −1/2

Integration, with log(1 − u/Pj ) = −u/Pj + O(Pj−2 ), proves (9.7).

    For the tail estimate write z = log(1 + v/Pj ). If 0 ≤ z ≤ Pj       , integration of the last slope
estimate gives ρ(xj + z) − ρ(xj ) ≥ Pj z/2, which is at least a fixed positive multiple of v in this
                 −1/2                                             p

For larger z, the contact inequality adds at least c(z − Pj              ). Since

                                      log(1 + v) ≤ log Pj + z + O(1)

and α0 < c, the increase is at least α0 log(1 + v) − O(1), uniformly for all v ≥ 1. Exponentiating

   Now suppose that p is bounded on contacts in a tail. Almost everywhere on the contact set the
derivatives of ρ and ρc agree, since the two locally Lipschitz functions agree there. Off that set
the derivative of ρc equals c. Consequently ρc has a uniform Lipschitz constant K1 on some tail.
Extend it to the left with a fixed Lipschitz constant. Consider the anchored translates

                                         u 7−→ ρc (x + u) − ρc (x).

They lie in a compact space of anchored Lipschitz functions, with the topology of uniform convergence
on compact intervals. Average their point masses over x ∈ [x0 , x0 + R]. A weak limit as R → ∞ is
invariant under every anchored translation

                                       (Th q)(u) = q(u + h) − q(h).

Indeed translating the averaging interval changes its normalized measure by at most 2|h|/R.

                                                      99
   The mean increment at 1 of this invariant law is at least 1. To check this, telescope the integral:
                      Z x0 +R                                    Z x0 +R+1          Z x0 +1    !
                  1                                     1
                                ρc (x + 1) − ρc (x) dx =                     ρc −             ρc ,
                  R    x0                                R        x0 +R              x0

and use ρc (x) ≥ x. Birkhoff’s pointwise ergodic theorem [Dur19, Theorem 6.2.1] for the invertible
map T1 , applied in both directions to the bounded unit increments, now provides a path q∗ in the
support of the law for which
                                q∗ (u)
                                       −→ λ ≥ 1          (u → +∞ or u → −∞).                         (9.13)
                                  u
The two limits agree because they are the same invariant conditional expectation of the unit
increment. Bounded increments between successive integers extend the conclusion to real u. Such a
path is approximated locally uniformly by translates with centers xj → ∞: a neighborhood visited
only for bounded x has zero mass in every limiting averaged measure.
    We next replace ρc by ρ. To the right of any fixed compact u-interval, (9.13) gives a fixed
interval [v, w] on which the increment of q∗ exceeds c(w − v). The approximating translates of ρc
must therefore have a contact in [xj + v, xj + w]. Monotonicity of ρ, together with ρ ≥ ρc , bounds
ρ(xj + u) − ρc (xj ) above and below on every compact interval. It also bounds the local slopes of ρ:
from (9.5),                          Z       x                                  
                                p(x) ≤ e         p(v) dv = e ρ(x) − ρ(x − 1) .
                                           x−1

Thus a subsequence of the anchored translates of ρ has a locally uniform limit ρ∗ , and ρ(xj )−ρc (xj ) =
O(1).
    For completeness, this limit has the same two-ended slope as q∗ . Fix a small η > 0. For every
sufficiently large |u|, the increment of q∗ on [u, u + η|u|] exceeds cη|u|, by (9.13) and λ > c. The
same contact argument, followed by passage to the limit, bounds the excess of ρ∗ (u) over q∗ (u),
up to a fixed additive constant, by K1 η|u|. The lower bound ρ ≥ ρc bounds the deficit by a fixed
constant. Letting η ↓ 0 proves (9.10).
    Finally, the lower increment bound for ρc , its uniform Lipschitz bound, and the bounded
difference at xj give (9.11) and (9.12) as long as v remains in the tail. For the remaining left range,
use ρ(v) ≥ v, increasing P ′ > max(K1 , 1) and the additive constant if necessary. The same Lipschitz
bound gives ρ(xj ) = O(1 + xj ).

9.2   The steep-contact alternative
Lemma 9.4. Alternative (i) of Theorem 9.3 is impossible for the moment law of a prohibited
sequence.

Proof. For each fixed j, take the original index n sufficiently large and set
                                                         dDj              B

For the last assertion, first keep j fixed: m0 ℓn = BDj mn (Dj )/Pj → ∞. The distance from hn to
the upper shape wall, in units of ℓn , is Pj → ∞; the lower wall also escapes in these units.

                                                       100
    Both (9.7) and (9.8) can be transferred to the actual moments on the required ranges. Here

cutoff Dcut large enough that the primary bound mn (D) ≤ D−1+εn is below a fixed multiple of the
right side of (9.8), with the normalizing factor mn (Dj ), whenever D ≥ Dcut . Such a cutoff exists
because 1 − εn > α0 and mn (Dj ) → m(Dj ) > 0. On the finite interval below the cutoff use locally
uniform moment convergence. Choose the fixed physical cutoff 0 < δb < H/2 small enough that the
enlarged depth range used for submean disks has logarithmic level at least k0 . Thus all the coarser
depths up to δb are controlled, with a constant independent of j.
    On the row of height hn , define

    Submean estimates applied before taking expectation give the same bounds for suprema on

of depth-sized tiles depending on L. The expected squared size of the fields on this band is at most

                                                                           

Since m0 ℓn → ∞, it follows that mx ℓn → ∞ in probability.
   Now consider the nonnegative subharmonic function

                                   Ux (S) = |eiS Fx (S)|2 + |e2iS Qx (S)|.

Its mean tends locally uniformly to 1. If a nonnegative smooth cutoff equals 1 on a fixed compact
set, integration against its Laplacian shows that the expected Riesz mass of Ux on that set tends to
zero: the limiting integral of the Laplacian of the cutoff is zero. The mass itself is nonnegative, so it
tends to zero in probability.

                                                          101
    We may therefore select deterministic centers and a subsequence with all the following properties:
local analytic bounds on an exhaustion of the plane; the bounds (9.16) for every fixed aperture;
mx ℓn → ∞; and vanishing Riesz mass on every fixed compact set. To make the quantifier explicit,
assign summable failure probabilities to countably increasing apertures and compact sets, choose
their bound constants once, and then take the sequence index sufficiently large for the remaining
probability conditions. The union of the bad events has probability less than one. This gives fixed
eventual constants for each aperture, not constants growing with the sequence index.
    The resulting entire limits (F , Q) obey

                                 |eiS F(S)|2 + |e2iS Q(S)|      harmonic.

Each summand is subharmonic, so each is harmonic. An analytic function with harmonic squared
modulus is constant. An analytic function with harmonic modulus is also constant: away from its
zeros its Laplacian is |G′ |2 /|G|, and the assertion follows by analytic continuation. Thus

                                 F(S) = c1 e−iS ,         Q(S) = c2 e−2iS .

At S = 0 the normalized sum is exactly 1, so the limit is nonzero. Moreover

                                          Fx′′ = (mx ℓn )2 Qx Fx .

Compact convergence and mx ℓn → ∞ imply QF = 0. The limit is consequently one of the two
nonzero pure exponential profiles in Theorem 7.1. All its finite-wall hypotheses were checked
above, including a single exponent α > 1/2 for every fixed aperture. That theorem gives the
contradiction.

We are therefore in alternative (ii) of Theorem 9.3. Choose its xj , ρ∗ , λ.

On a finite v-range, the limiting estimates (9.11) and (9.12) imply an upper exponent 1 − c for

For fixed j, the last expression tends to infinity. We can therefore make it at least one before

of P . Thus the coarse hypotheses of the weighted bootstrap hold with constants independent of j.
The global envelope already has this uniformity, so the resulting weighted L2 bounds do as well.
    For each fixed j, the new q-field on a fixed compact set is the primary q-field on another
fixed compact set, multiplied by the finite factor mn (exj )−2 . The primary convergence to zero in
ν-probability can therefore be imposed to any prescribed tolerance before advancing j. Hence the
new q-field tends to zero in probability on compact sets.
    Weighted submean estimates now give tightness of the analytic fields on every compact subset of
H− . They also give pointwise cone tightness. At depth ratio D ≥ 1, the coarse part of (9.19) bounds
the weighted field norm by CD−(1−β) . Summing depth-sized tile suprema over dyadic bands, with
any fixed α ∈ (1/2, 1 − β), proves tightness of the constants in (9.20). Fine tiles use the bounded
change of the density of ν on shifts of order the ancestor depth. For tiles coarser than that depth,
the necessary tile estimates are precisely the explicit coarse hypotheses of the weighted bootstrap.
Thus no translation invariance of ν is required.


                                                  103
    Choose deterministic centers on events with these compact and cone bounds and with the q-field
small, using summable exceptional probabilities as in the preceding proof. Extract a locally uniform
limit (F, 0). If F were zero, its compact smallness and the coarser bound with exponent α > 1/2
would violate Theorem 8.6; the global normalization at the target is B ′ = µ(K), as that theorem
requires. Therefore F ̸≡ 0.
    Each quotient Fn′′ /Fn = (d′ )2 q is holomorphic. Around any zero of F , choose a small circle
containing no other zero and no zero on the circle. The quotients converge on that circle to F ′′ /F ,
so maximum modulus bounds them in its interior. Their limit removes the apparent singularity
of F ′′ /F . This proves the last asserted regularity. In particular all zeros of F are simple and F ′′
vanishes at them: otherwise the analytic equation F ′′ = (F ′′ /F )F with initial data F = F ′ = 0
would force F ≡ 0.

eventually for each fixed D. Recall that ∆(t) = H − hX (t). This is the point where a successful
local passage is converted to a full horizontal domain; local analytic continuation alone would not
give (9.29).
    The full-width domains in (9.29) exhaust (9.27), with a strict margin on compact sets. The shape
constraint gives ℑSn < 0, and the deep anchor gives a fixed interior normalization. Holomorphic
maps to a half-plane with such an anchor form a normal family, as is seen by mapping the half-plane
to the unit disk. Every limit agrees with the unique deep solution. This defines S(T, ·) on (9.27); the
energy identity propagates there by the identity theorem. The same argument for a sequence of real
parameters Tj → T proves local uniform continuity: on a common strict interior, every convergent
subsequence has the same deep germ and hence the same limit. This also treats T = 0.
    It remains to justify joint germs at all real parameters. Equation (9.26) first shows that J ̸= 0.
At fixed w = T F (S), its J-derivative is

                                     ∂                    sin z
                                       J 3 g(wJ/2) = 3J 2
                                                  
                                                                .                               (9.30)
                                    ∂J                      z
It can vanish only when z = nπ, n ∈ Z \ {0}. The equivalent entire equation

                                   (T F (S))3 = 24(sin z − z cos z)                             (9.31)

then has a nonzero right side. Differentiating in Y gives

                                  3T 3 F (S)2 F ′ (S)J = 24z sin z zY ,

                                                  106
and hence F ′ (S) = 0 at such a point. The function F is nonconstant, because it is nonzero and
decays down the cones. Its critical points are therefore countable. At each such point and each
nonzero integer n, (9.31) allows at most three values of T . Thus the exceptional real parameters
form at most a countable set.
    This countable exception is removable. Fix an exceptional real T , a strict interior point, and a
nearby sequence of nonexceptional real parameters. On a common connected interior Y -domain,
continuity gives local uniform convergence of z(Y, Tj ) to z(Y, T ). For each fixed nonzero integer n,
Hurwitz’s theorem says that z(Y, T ) − nπ is either zero-free or identically zero. The latter alternative
is impossible on the common domain: analytic continuation to its deep part gives z → 0. Exhausting
the strict interior proves omission of all the exceptional values for every real T .
    Consequently (9.30) is nonzero along every compact vertical path in the real-parameter domain.
Solve the regular implicit equation as an analytic first-order ODE in Y , starting with the joint
deep data. A finite chain of local ODE neighborhoods along the path gives a joint germ in (T, Y )
at its endpoint. The constructed germ agrees with the given real-parameter functions by their
continuity and local ODE uniqueness. Germs obtained on overlapping vertical paths agree by the
same uniqueness, which proves (iv).

9.5   Complex parameter disks and the growth estimate
The real domains in (9.27) have a cusp at T = 0. We fill graphs over a fixed parameter half-disk to
produce a parameter disk of radius comparable to D1/r at a phase point of depth D. The shape
constraint on this disk will bound the second parameter coefficient.

Lemma 9.7 (Holomorphic filling of a moving graph). Let Γ be a piecewise smooth Jordan curve,
and let U be a connected open neighborhood of a compact path in a complex variable w. Suppose
A(T, w) is holomorphic on a neighborhood of Γ × U . If, for w in some nonempty open subset of
U , it extends holomorphically to the interior of Γ, then it has such an extension for every w ∈ U ,
jointly holomorphic in T, w. The extension agrees with the original collar germs.

Proof. For every integer n ≥ 0, the moment
                                           Z
                                                T n A(T, w) dT
                                            Γ

is holomorphic in w. It vanishes in the initial open set and hence throughout U . For an exterior
point z of large modulus, expand (T − z)−1 in its geometric series. The vanishing moments show
that the exterior Cauchy transform is zero there, and analytic continuation makes it zero on the
whole exterior. The interior Cauchy integral

                                           1      A(T, w)
                                                Z
                                                          dT
                                          2πi    Γ T −z

is jointly holomorphic in z, w. Deforming the contour a short distance inside and outside any collar
point and using the zero exterior transform shows that it agrees there with A. It is the required
extension.
