Proposition 9.8 (Growth of the limiting first field). For every compact horizontal interval I ⊂ R
and every ε > 0, there is a constant CI,ε such that

                         |F (x − iD)| ≤ CI,ε D−λ−ε       (x ∈ I, 0 < D < 1).                   (9.32)

Equivalently,
                           sup log |F (x − ie−v )| ≤ λv + o(v)       (v → ∞),                  (9.33)
                           x∈I

where the error is uniform on I.

Proof. Fix ε > 0, use Theorem 9.6 with r = 1/(λ + ε) ∈ (0, 1), and take a small fixed upper half-disk
|T | < t0 , ℑT > 0. For a target Y with ℜY ∈ I, −ℑY = D, and for |Tc | ≤ c∗ D1/r in this half-disk,
use the graph with phase argument

                Y + G(T ) − G(Tc ),     G(T ) = −iC∗ (η0 − iT )r ,      0 < η0 ≤ c∗ D1/r .     (9.34)

The fractional power is the branch on the right half-plane; ℜ(η0 − iT ) > 0 in the upper half-disk.
The auxiliary η0 is distinct from the fixed growth exponent ε.
   On the real diameter,

                           −ℑG(T ) = C∗ ℜ(η0 − iT )r ≥ C∗ cos(πr/2)|T |r .

Choose C∗ large compared with the constant in (9.27), and then c∗ so small that |G(Tc )| < D/2.
Every diameter point then lies strictly in its real-parameter domain. On the semicircle |T | = t0 ,
ℑT ≥ 0,
                       −ℑG(T ) ≥ cr C∗ tr0 ,     |ℜG(T )| ≤ Cr (−ℑG(T )).
Fix the required cone aperture first and then increase C∗ further to put this semicircle in the deep
joint domain. The bounded interval I and the correction G(Tc ) cost only fixed additional margins.
    The graph has coherent joint germs on a boundary collar. On the diameter these are the germs
of Theorem 9.6(iv); on the semicircle they are deep joint data. They agree at the corners because
both are attached to the same deep branch. Overlapping real-parameter neighborhoods agree first
for real parameters and then by holomorphic identity.
    Lower the center Y until the whole graph lies in deep joint data, then raise it vertically to the
target. Boundary membership and strict margins persist. On each compact center segment a finite
covering gives a holomorphic function on a neighborhood of the boundary contour times a connected
complex neighborhood of that segment. Coherence follows from vertical-path uniqueness. Initially
the graph interior is filled by deep data, so Theorem 9.7 fills it throughout the motion. Its boundary
values have negative imaginary part; the harmonic maximum principle therefore gives ℑS < 0 on
the filled interior.
    For the lower half-disk use G(T ) = −iC∗ (η0 + iT )r . The two constructions agree on the joint
real-parameter germs. The germs obtained for different Tc also agree on overlaps: at a fixed
parameter they follow the same vertical path from the unique deep branch. Thus the model is
holomorphic on the whole parameter disk

                                             |T | < c∗ D1/r                                    (9.35)

at the target, still with ℑS < 0. Shrinking c∗ once makes this construction uniform for Y in a disk
of radius a small fixed multiple of D.
    Write
                                  S(T, Y ) = Y + T 2 A(Y ) + O(T 4 ).

                                                  108
Evenness follows from the deep branch and continuation. The positive harmonic function −ℑS(T, Y )
equals D at T = 0. Its Fourier coefficient estimate on circles in (9.35), or the Caratheodory coefficient
estimate, gives
                                          |A(Y )| ≤ CD1−2/r .
This also holds on the depth-sized Y -neighborhood, so Cauchy’s estimate yields

                                           |A′ (Y )| ≤ CD−2/r .                                     (9.36)

The energy equation determines the derivative:

9.6    Uniform high-value witnesses
Set
                                          L(v) = log m∗ (e−v ).                                     (9.38)
By (9.18), L(v) = λv + o(|v|) at both ends.

Lemma 9.9 (Common normal epochs). Fix
                               1
                                 < c1 < 1 − β,        P1 > max(λ, 1 + a).
                               2
There is an unbounded set E ⊂ (0, ∞), with dist(v, E) = o(v) as v → ∞, such that every u ∈ E
satisfies, for all real v,                 (
                                             c1 (v − u), v ≤ u,
                             L(v) − L(u) ≤                                             (9.39)
                                             P1 (v − u), v ≥ u.

Proof. The coarse part of (9.19) gives L(v) ≤ C + (1 − β)v for v ≤ 0. Thus L(v) − c1 v → −∞ as
v → −∞. For a large w, choose a running record u0 ≤ w of L(v) − c1 v on (−∞, w]. Its positive
asymptotic slope λ − c1 implies w − u0 = o(w): for every fixed η > 0, all values at v ≤ (1 − η)w are
eventually below the value at w.
   Next maximize L(v) − P1 v over v ≥ u0 , and call a maximizer u. It exists because P1 > λ. The
negative asymptotic slope shows u − u0 = o(u0 ). Its maximality gives the future bound in (9.39).
For v ≤ u0 , the past record and L(u) − L(u0 ) ≥ P1 (u − u0 ) ≥ c1 (u − u0 ) give the past bound at u.
For u0 ≤ v ≤ u, maximality gives

                                 L(v) − L(u) ≤ P1 (v − u) ≤ c1 (v − u).

The epochs obtained this way are within o(w) of every sufficiently large w.

argument in that theorem is contradictory.

uniformly over x ∈ I. A decreasing diagonal choice of η yields ηI (u) → 0 in (9.40).

                                                  111
9.7    The high-modulus mesh contradiction
We isolate the function-theoretic consequence of the growth bound and the common-epoch witnesses.
In the following argument a depth is the positive number δ = −ℑz, and its logarithmic depth is
v = − log δ.

Theorem 9.11 (High-modulus mesh contradiction). Let F ̸≡ 0 be holomorphic in H− := {z ∈ C :
ℑz < 0}, and suppose that F ′′ /F extends holomorphically to H− . There cannot exist a number
λ ≥ 1 and an unbounded set of epochs E ⊂ (0, ∞) with all of the following properties.
  (i) For every compact interval I ⊂ R,

                                sup log |F (x − ie−v )| ≤ λv + o(v),           v −→ ∞.                (9.48)
                                x∈I

      The error is uniform in x ∈ I.
 (ii) The epochs are relatively dense in the sense that

                                          dist(v, E) = o(v),        v −→ ∞.                           (9.49)

 (iii) For every compact interval I, there are a fixed aperture AI < ∞ and a function rI (u) → 0 such
       that, for every sufficiently large u ∈ E and every x ∈ I, there is a point w = p − iδ satisfying

              |p − x| ≤ AI δ,         |− log δ − u| ≤ rI (u)u,          log |F (w)| ≥ λu − rI (u)u.   (9.50)

In particular, the epochs in (iii) are common to all the centers in I; no choice of an epoch depending
on the center is allowed.

    We first prove the two local estimates used to construct the mesh. All references to Brownian
motion below can equivalently be formulated using harmonic measure and the minimum principle
for superharmonic functions.

Lemma 9.12 (Small shadows of a high-deficit set). Fix constants 1 ≤ a ≤ b and K > 0. Let
w = p − id, and let T be a finite family of squares T of sides ρT and center depths dT , with

                       aρT ≤ dT ≤ bρT ,             dT ≤ d/2,        |ℜcT − p| ≤ Kd.

Choose a rectangle
                                R = {z : |ℜz − p| < M d,         τ < −ℑz < 2d},
where M is sufficiently large in terms of a, b, K, and 0 < τ < 14 minT dT . Suppose that U ≥ 0 is
superharmonic in a neighborhood of R, that U (w) < ∞, and that U ≥ H > 0 on the concentric
disks DT of radius ρT /8. If IT is the horizontal projection of T , then

                                              [               U (w)
                                                    IT ≤ Cd         .                                 (9.51)
                                             T ∈T
                                                               H

Here C depends only on the fixed geometric constants.

Proof. Let B be Brownian motion started at w, stopped when it exits R. Superharmonic comparison,
applied first up to the first hit of the union of the disks and then up to the exit, gives

                         Pw {B hits some DT before exiting R} ≤ U (w)/H.                              (9.52)


                                                      112
This comparison allows logarithmic poles of U : one can first stop away from those poles and then use
monotone exhaustion. Alternatively, the harmonic probability of hitting the disk union, multiplied
by H, is a subfunction of U by the minimum principle on the complement of the disks.
   For each T , consider the event that the motion hits DT and subsequently exits R through its
top side at a horizontal coordinate in IT . We claim that its probability is at least cρT /d. First
stop at the horizontal line of depth dT , or at the other sides of the part of R below that line. The
harmonic measure density on the segment

                                 |x − ℜcT | < ρT /32,      −ℑz = dT ,

is bounded below by c/d. To see the uniformity, divide all lengths by d. The starting point has
depth one, the bottom has depth two, and the new top has depth in [0, 1/2]; the segment lies a fixed
positive distance from the lateral sides. The interior-to-side Poisson density of a rectangle is positive
and continuous in these parameters, including top depth zero. Its minimum on this compact family
is therefore positive. Thus the probability of this first hit inside DT is at least cρT /d.
     From any point of that segment, the probability of exiting the original rectangle through its
top within IT has a fixed positive lower bound. Indeed, use a smaller rectangle with horizontal
center ℜcT , horizontal half-width a fixed large multiple of dT , top depth τ , and bottom depth 2dT .
It lies in R after increasing M . After division by dT , its starting points, top depth in [0, 1/4], and
target intervals of length ρT /dT ∈ [1/b, 1/a] again range over a compact family with nondegenerate
margins. Positivity and continuity of harmonic measure give the claimed lower bound. Exiting
this smaller rectangle on the specified top interval also exits R there. The strong Markov property
proves the claim.
     Choose a subfamily of the intervals IT with disjoint interiors whose total length is at least one
third of the length of their union. For completeness, order the finite family by decreasing length,
retain an interval if its interior is disjoint from those already retained, and discard it otherwise.
Each discarded interval is contained in the threefold concentric enlargement of an intersecting
retained interval of at least its length. This proves the asserted covering estimate. The events just
constructed for the retained intervals are disjoint, because their final exit coordinates are in disjoint
intervals; individual endpoints have harmonic measure zero. Each event is contained in the event in
(9.52). Summing their lower bounds gives (9.51). In particular, no union bound over the number of
tiles is being used.

There is nothing to prove when N = 0. Otherwise, consider all disks B(z, hi/N ) whose interiors
contain at least i of the listed points, for 1 ≤ i ≤ N . Starting with the largest of the finitely many

already selected. Only finitely many disks can be selected: their centers lie in a bounded set and
their radii are at least h/N . Each unselected disk intersects a selected one of at least its radius, and
is contained in that disk’s threefold enlargement. If the selected radii are hik /N , disjoint interiors
show that k ik ≤ N , by counting the points they contain. The enlarged disks therefore have total
            P

radius at most 3h. Outside them, the i-th smallest distance to the points is at least hi/N , for every

                             U ≤ CT + N log(e/h) ≤ Ch T                on B(c, 10ρ).

    It remains to justify actual routing, including at corner contacts. If Q, Q′ touch and have
comparable sizes, choose a square centered at a contact point of side min(ρQ , ρQ′ ). This square lies
in Q∗ ∩ (Q′ )∗ . The sums of the lengths of the horizontal and vertical projections of all exceptional
disks for both tiles are at most Ch(ρQ + ρQ′ ). Fix h so small that each of these sums is less than
half the side of the contact square. One can then choose an x-coordinate and a y-coordinate in that
square, each outside the corresponding projections of both disk families. Their intersection is a
junction point safe for both tiles. Choose the two endpoint coordinates in the same fashion in the
prescribed endpoint squares. Within a tile, join its two chosen points by one horizontal and one
vertical segment. Both coordinates at each endpoint avoid all exceptional projections for that tile,
so these segments avoid all of its exceptional disks. They lie in Q∗ , and their combined length is at
most CρQ . Concatenation proves the routing assertion. The same construction works when a chain
consists of a single tile.

                                                      114
Proof of Theorem 9.11. We work in fixed horizontal intervals contained in [−5, 5]. All errors below
are uniform on these intervals. Fix the aperture from (9.50) for this compact interval.

Separated bands with neighboring logarithmic depths. Choose a nonincreasing function
θ(v) → 0, with θ(v) ≥ v −1/4 , whose square bounds, eventually, the relative errors in (9.49) and
(9.50), uniformly for all arguments at least v. Such a majorant is obtained by taking the decreasing
tail supremum of these errors, its square root, and the maximum with v −1/4 . Enlarging it slightly
accommodates approximate nearest epochs if E is not closed.
    Starting at a sufficiently large u1 ∈ E, set sj = θ(uj )uj , and choose

                                uj+1 ∈ E,      uj+1 = uj + 8sj + o(sj ).                           (9.56)

Indeed the error in finding an epoch near uj + 8sj is at most Cθ(uj )2 uj = o(sj ). The monotonicity
of θ implies sj+1 ≤ (1 + o(1))sj . Consequently the bands [uj − sj , uj + sj ] are disjoint, their gaps
tend to infinity, sj → ∞, and

                                uj+k = uj + o(uj )       (k = −1, 0, 1, 2).                        (9.57)

Every witness at epoch uj has logarithmic depth uj + o(sj ). These statements survive any fixed
additive changes to the ends of the logarithmic bands.
   Choose dyadic depths aj , bj with

                       aj = e−uj −sj +O(1) ,    bj = e−uj +sj +O(1) ,         aj < bj .

The horizontal rectangle is Rj = [−3, 3]×[aj , bj ] in the coordinates (x, δ). The two vertical rectangles
have horizontal intervals [−2, −3/2] and [3/2, 2], and depth interval [aj+2 /8, 8bj ]; dyadic rounding
can be incorporated into these fixed factors. The factors eight leave room at the ends for all
subsequent local enlargements. A witness at epoch uj−1 is deeper than the entire vertical rectangle
by a factor tending to infinity. In fact, the logarithmic gap from that witness to its bottom is at
least (7 − o(1))sj−1 .
     Take a slightly larger open region containing these three rectangles, the witnesses at epochs
uj , uj−1 needed for centers in [−3, 3], and the fixed-factor boxes that will be used about them. Its
horizontal coordinates lie in [−5, 5]; all its logarithmic depths are uj + o(uj ), by (9.57). By (9.48)
we can choose
                    Mj = λuj + o(uj ),        Mj ≥ λuj ,       Uj = Mj − log |F | ≥ 0               (9.58)
throughout this region. The function Uj is superharmonic and may have logarithmic poles at the
zeros of F . Its value at every witness just specified is o(uj ), uniformly in its center.

Tiles and the exclusion of bad barriers. Fix a sufficiently large dyadic integer N0 . In the
layer 2−l−1 ≤ δ ≤ 2−l , tile by squares of side ρ = 2−l−1 /N0 , using the common dyadic horizontal
grid. The sides of a tile are thus a small fixed fraction of its depth. Take N0 large enough that
the disks of radius 100ρ required in Theorem 9.13 lie within the fixed-factor enlargements already
allowed. The rectangles have dyadic boundaries, so they are finite unions of these tiles. Tiles that
touch, even at a corner, have side ratios between one half and two.
    Choose εj → 0 so slowly that the uniform witness deficits in (9.58) are o(εj uj ), and so that
Mj − λuj = o(εj uj ). Color a tile good when

                                       sup log |F | ≥ λuj − εj uj ,                                (9.59)
                                         T


                                                   115
and bad otherwise. On every bad tile, Uj ≥ εj uj . For any witness w at one of the two indicated
epochs, Theorem 9.12 therefore implies that the union of the projections of bad tiles with center
depth at most δw /2 and horizontal center distance at most Kδw from w has length

                                                 o(δw ).                                           (9.60)

Here K is any fixed constant needed below. For this application put the fictitious top of the
Brownian rectangle a fixed factor shallower than the shallowest tile under consideration. The
rectangle then lies in the enlarged region of (9.58).
    There is no bad chain in Rj from its shallow edge to its deep edge, even when corner contacts
are allowed. Suppose such a chain exists and let x0 be a point where it meets the shallow edge.
Choose the epoch-uj witness p − iδw for the center x0 . The shallow edge is much shallower than δw ,
and the deep edge is much deeper. Follow the chain from its shallow end until it first reaches depth
δw /4 or first leaves the horizontal interval [x0 − δw , x0 + δw ]. Taking N0 large guarantees that all
tiles up to and including this first event have center depths at most δw /2 and center distances from
p at most (AI + 2)δw . In the first case the last tile has side at least cδw /N0 , so its shadow alone has
length at least a fixed positive multiple of δw . In the second case the projections of this connected
chain prefix cover an interval of length at least cδw . Both conclusions contradict (9.60).
    Neither vertical rectangle has a bad chain joining its two lateral sides. Choose an epoch-uj−1
witness for the midpoint of its fixed horizontal interval. This witness has depth δw , and every tile
of the rectangle has center depth less than δw /2 for large j. Its horizontal coordinate p lies in
the interior of the strip, since its distance from the midpoint is at most AI δw → 0. A lateral bad
crossing projects onto the whole strip. The tiles of that crossing with centers in [p − 2δw , p + 2δw ]
therefore have projections covering [p − δw , p + δw ], once N0 is large. This again contradicts (9.60).

Planar duality and routing the good chains. We include the finite grid argument to specify
the corner convention. Subdivide every tile of a rectangle to its finest dyadic square grid, retaining
the parent tile’s color. On a finite square grid, either there is a good left-to-right path using shared
edges, or there is a bad top-to-bottom path using shared edges or corners. To prove this, mark all
good cells reachable by shared edges from the left side and adjoin an exterior collar along the left. If
this set reaches the right, the first alternative holds. Otherwise follow its right-hand boundary from
the top of the rectangle to the bottom, with the marked set on the left. At vertices with several
possible continuations, keep the marked set on the left, equivalently resolve the vertex by a small
rounded arc. Closed boundary components can be ignored. The cells immediately on the right of
the resulting boundary are bad: an edge-adjacent good cell would have been marked. Consecutive
such cells share an edge or a corner, and they join the top to the bottom. If no cell was marked,
the first column itself is such a bad chain. This proves the alternative. Interchanging the two
coordinate directions gives its vertical version. Mapping a chain back to its parent tiles preserves
corner adjacency; removing loops and repeated tiles gives a simple parent chain.
    The bad barriers have already been excluded. Thus Rj has a good horizontal chain, and
each vertical rectangle has a good vertical chain. On a good tile the infimum of Uj is at most
Mj −λuj +εj uj = o(uj ), uniformly. Apply Theorem 9.13 to route each chain. We obtain a horizontal
path γj , extending from near −3 to near 3, and vertical paths σj− , σj+ in slight enlargements of the
two side strips, with
                               log |F | ≥ λuj − o(uj ) on γj ∪ σj− ∪ σj+ .                         (9.61)
The horizontal path stays in a fixed-factor enlargement of its band. The vertical paths start deeper
than all of γj and end shallower than all of γj+2 . They remain to the left or right, respectively, of
the entire interval [−1, 1].

                                                  116
    The length estimate in the routing lemma is subexponential in uj . Indeed, in any of these
rectangles the smallest tile side is e−uj +o(uj ) , the largest side is e−uj +o(uj ) , its height is e−uj +o(uj ) ,
and its width is bounded. Even its entire finest grid contains at most euj +o(uj ) cells. Hence the
number of parent tiles times their largest side, and therefore each routed path’s length, is at most
                                                  exp(o(uj )).                                              (9.62)
All local constants, including those in the Cartan covering, have been fixed before j tends to infinity.
    Each vertical path intersects each of γj , γj+1 , γj+2 . One elementary way to verify this is to erase
loops from a horizontal polygonal path and trim it at two vertical lines outside the side strips. It is
then a simple arc joining the opposite sides of a rectangle, confined to its horizontal band. Closing
this arc along three sides of the rectangle and applying the Jordan curve theorem separates points
above the band from points below it. A vertical path in either side strip has endpoints on these
different sides and must meet the arc. The fixed-factor margins ensure that local routing has not
changed this separation.

A primitive with no logarithmic monodromy. Set q = F ′′ /F , holomorphic by hypothesis.
Every zero z0 of F is simple. Otherwise F (z0 ) = F ′ (z0 ) = 0, and uniqueness for the holomorphic
equation F ′′ = qF would imply F ≡ 0. At a simple zero this equation also gives F ′′ (z0 ) = 0, and
hence
           F (z) = a(z − z0 ) + O((z − z0 )3 ),        F (z)−2 = a−2 (z − z0 )−2 + O(1),        a ̸= 0.
In particular all residues of F −2 vanish. Integration around any closed curve avoiding its poles is
zero: the curve is compact in the simply connected half-plane and encloses only finitely many poles,
so this is the residue theorem. Thus there is a single-valued meromorphic function C0 in H− with
                                                   C0′ = F −2 .                                             (9.63)
Its singularity at a zero z0 is −a−2 (z − z0 )−1 .
    The paths have no zeros, by (9.61). Equations (9.61)–(9.62) imply that the oscillation of C0
along any one of the three paths with index j is at most
                                                  e−2λuj +o(uj ) .                                          (9.64)
Choose an arbitrary point zj ∈ γj . The connections just proved join zj to zj+1 by pieces of
γj , σj− , γj+1 . Thus |C0 (zj+1 ) − C0 (zj )| ≤ e−2λuj +o(uj ) . The gaps uj+1 − uj eventually exceed one.
For any fixed η ∈ (0, 2λ) and all sufficiently large j, the telescoping tail is therefore bounded by
                                                              e−(2λ−η)uj
                                             e−(2λ−η)uk ≤
                                       X

                                       k≥j

It follows that C0 (zj ) tends to a constant c∗ . Letting η tend to zero, and using (9.64) to reach every
point of the three paths, gives the uniform estimate
                                |C0 − c∗ | ≤ e−2λuj +o(uj )    on γj ∪ σj− ∪ σj+ .                          (9.65)
This is one common constant for the whole mesh, not a separate constant for each band or each
side strip.

Figure 4: The mesh in the pure first-field model. Vertical spacing is schematic: the bands lie
at logarithmic depths uj + o(uj ). Horizontal paths and side connectors enclose every sufficiently
shallow point of a smaller interval. Their high-modulus bounds give one common limit c∗ for the
meromorphic primitive C0 , where C0′ = F −2 . The product G = F (C0 − c∗ ) is holomorphic even at
zeros of F ; its boundary estimate therefore passes to the enclosed regions by the maximum principle.


Enclosing every shallow point and reflecting. The arrangement of the paths is illustrated in
Figure 4. Write [αj , βj ] for a logarithmic-depth interval containing the entire routed horizontal
path γj . The fixed-factor enlargements give αj = uj − sj − O(1) and βj = uj + sj + O(1), and
βj < αj+1 for large j. Given z = x − ie−v with x ∈ [−1, 1] and v large, choose j so that

                                            βj < v ≤ βj+1 .

Then γj lies strictly below z, whereas γj+2 lies strictly above z. The two paths σj− and σj+ connect
across both bands and lie strictly to the left and right of z.
     Choose an intersection of each side path with the lower and upper horizontal paths, and use
the corresponding path segments to form a closed walk, in the order lower, right, upper, left. More
explicitly, along either side path take its last lower-path hit before its first subsequent upper-path
hit; its intervening segment then joins those two paths. Join these endpoints along the horizontal
paths. Every piece lies in the indicated open half-plane relative to z, and the four corner points
lie in the corresponding quadrants. This walk has winding number one about z: replace each of
its pieces by a straight segment within its open half-plane, which is convex and avoids z. The
resulting quadrilateral traverses successively the four quadrants and has total change of argument
2π. Equivalently, the arguments on the lower, right, upper, and left pieces can be chosen in their
respective half-plane intervals and then joined in this order. Self-intersections of a path or of the
walk do not affect this winding calculation.
     The complementary component containing z is therefore bounded and has boundary in this walk.
It lies compactly in the half-plane. The maximum principle for the holomorphic function G gives its
bound there from the walk. The upper horizontal piece has index j + 2, and uj+2 = uj + o(uj ), so
(9.67) yields
                                     |G(x − ie−v )| ≤ e−λuj +o(uj ) .
The same estimate is immediate if the point lies on a mesh path. As v → ∞, the selected j tends to
infinity uniformly for x ∈ [−1, 1]. Hence G has continuous boundary value zero on (−1, 1), uniformly
on compact subintervals.
    Schwarz reflection across this interval extends G holomorphically by G(z). The extension
vanishes on the interval, so the identity theorem gives G ≡ 0. On any disk where F ̸= 0, this says
C0 ≡ c∗ , contradicting C0′ = F −2 ̸= 0. The contradiction proves the Theorem.


                                                  118
Proof of Theorem 9.1. Assume that a sequence in Theorem 8.7 exists. Its primary moments have a
limit by Theorem 9.2. Apply Theorem 9.3 to its logarithmic law. The steep-contact alternative
contradicts Theorem 7.1, as proved in Theorem 9.4. The bounded-contact alternative gives the
fields in Theorem 9.5. Their nonzero first field has holomorphic quotient F ′′ /F . The limiting
impact equation and parameter continuation, Theorems 9.6 and 9.8, give its uniform upper growth.
Theorems 9.9 and 9.10 give common relatively dense epochs and uniform witnesses at every horizontal
center. These are precisely the hypotheses contradicted by Theorem 9.11. Both alternatives are
impossible, proving the Theorem.

Corollary 9.14 (The critical angle budget is available). In the remaining finite-wall critical case,
the angle budget of Theorem 8.11 and the polynomial bounds of Theorem 8.9 hold unconditionally.
In particular, for some ϵ > 0,     Z     K
                                             µf (k)2 dk ≤ CK 2−ϵ .
                                        k0

Proof. The sole forward assumption in the critical reduction was the nonexistence of the sequences
in Theorem 8.7. This is Theorem 9.1. Apply the two stated results. Their conclusions were not
used anywhere in the proof of that theorem.


10     An odd periodic slice and its stopped geometry
We now work in the remaining nonisotropic case: the shape strip has finite width H, and the growth
at its boundary is critical. By Theorem 9.14, there are constants 0 < ρ < 1 and C such that
                               Z K
                                     µf (v)2 dv ≤ CK 2ρ        (K ≥ k0 ).                      (10.1)
                                k0

We shall use this saving to control the geometry of one fixed periodic orbit family, even when its
complex continuation approaches the shape wall. The estimates will hold up to a stopping time
defined by the greatest logarithmic shape depth visited so far. Keeping this history in the stopping
rule is essential: it permits us to use a finite collection of determinant estimates when the sites
requiring those estimates change along a path.

The chord lengths ℓj are directed meromorphic lengths; they are positive on the real collar.

               pj−1 + pj = 2Hj0 cos d0j ,                            pj − pj−1 = 2(Xj · eψj ) sin d0j ,   (10.5)

                               f (s) = a(s − s0 ) + O((s − s0 )3 ),                a ̸= 0.

Thus ϕ(s) − ϕ(s0 ) = O((s − s0 )2 ), and the residue of Γ is parallel to eϕ(s0 ) . Its scalar product with
nϕ(s) therefore has no pole.

                                                           120
Lemma 10.1 (Odd-cycle matrices). The following identities hold meromorphically on the slice:

                                    Hw = 0,        2fj s′j = (M0 w)j .                            (10.10)

On the real collar, wj > 0, H is negative semidefinite, and each of its proper principal submatrices
is negative definite. The matrix M0 is positive definite there.
    More generally, let I be a proper subset of the sites and let F ⊆ I c . Retain the sites of I with
their actual curvature, retain the sites of F with curvature zero, and delete all other sites. The
resulting matrix                                                    
                               BI,F = (M0 )I∪F,I∪F − diag Vi 1{i∈I}                            (10.11)
is invertible on the real collar. In particular its meromorphic determinant is not identically zero.
For an empty retained set we use determinant 1 and inverse norm 0.

Proof. First work on the real collar. If δarcj is an arc-length variation at vertex j, put zj =
sin d0j δarcj . The normal projections on edge j of the two endpoint variations are zj and −zj+1 . The
second variation of that edge length is therefore (zj + zj+1 )2 /ℓj , apart from the acceleration of the
endpoint paths. The latter acceleration is −nψj fj3 /c per squared arc-length increment. Pairing it
with the length gradient eθj−1 − eθj gives −2fj3 sin d0j /c per squared arc-length increment, or −Vj zj2 .
Thus H is the length Hessian in these variables. Differentiation along the periodic family gives
Hw = 0. Since Vj wj = 2fj s′j , this also gives the second identity in Equation (10.10). Analytic
continuation proves the meromorphic identities.
    The real collar is sufficiently small that s′j > 0 and sin d0j > 0, so wj > 0. Let Dw = diag(wj )
and put γj = wj wj+1 /ℓj > 0. The off-diagonal entries of Dw HDw are γj ; the identity Hw = 0 fixes
its diagonal entries as −γj−1 − γj . Consequently
                                                      X
                                 ⟨Dw HDw z, z⟩ = −          γj (zj − zj+1 )2 .
                                                        j

The cycle is connected. Its only null vectors are constant, and fixing any one coordinate to zero
removes the nullspace. This proves the two claims concerning H. A null vector for M0 would satisfy
zj+1 = −zj for every j; oddness then forces z = 0.
    For the last assertion, arrange the retained coordinates as I, F . The truthful diagonal block
is HI,I < 0, and the flat diagonal block is (M0 )F,F > 0. When both are nonempty, the Schur
complement of the flat block is

                                 HI,I − (M0 )I,F (M0 )−1
                                                      F,F (M0 )F,I < 0.

Hence the full matrix is invertible. If one of the two blocks is empty, one of the already proved
definite-matrix statements applies.

For completeness, write the positive Laplacian as an incidence matrix, times diag(γi ), times its

10.2    Contact with the shape wall
Theorem 10.3 (Boundary contact). At almost every point of either horizontal boundary of the
phase strip |ℑy| < h, the functions s0 , . . . , sN −1 have finite nontangential limits, and at least one of
these limits lies on |ℑs| = H.

Proof. By Theorem 5.4, each eisj is bounded and bounded away from zero. The boundary limit
theorem for bounded holomorphic functions gives nontangential limits almost everywhere [Gar07,
Chapter I, Theorem 5.3]. Near a nonzero limiting value, choose a local logarithm. On a connected
truncated approach cone it differs from isj by one fixed integral multiple of 2πi. Thus sj itself has a
finite nontangential limit.
    Suppose the limiting states are all strictly inside the shape strip on a set of positive measure. At
a point of this set we can require, in addition, that every fj , sin d0j , ℓj , and det Hjb have a nonzero
limit. To justify the requirement, first exclude limiting zeros of the fj . Each fj is holomorphic on

                                                          122
the phase strip and nonzero on the real collar. If its angular limit were zero on a positive-measure
subset, the same boundary uniqueness theorem used below would force fj ≡ 0, a contradiction.
Thus the finite union of these exceptional sets has measure zero. The sine and length evaluations
are then regular at the interior limiting state tuple. Exclude their zero limits next; all cofactor
evaluations are regular after those exclusions. If any one of these meromorphic functions had limiting
value zero on a positive-measure set, Privalov’s nontangential boundary uniqueness theorem for
meromorphic functions would make it identically zero [Gar07, Chapter II, p. 91]. This is impossible
by its nondegenerate real values. There are only finitely many functions to exclude. To apply the
disk boundary theorems to this strip, restrict to a compact boundary subarc of positive measure
and use a conformal map to the disk. The map extends analytically across that subarc with nonzero
derivative, preserving null sets and nontangential approach regions.
   Write s = (s0 , . . . , sN −1 ). Near such a limiting tuple the cofactor identity and Equation (10.9)
express
                                                (s′j )2 = Gj (s),
where each Gj is analytic and nonzero. Choose the square roots with the signs of the actual functions
on a truncated approach cone. We obtain a regular analytic vector equation s′ = g(s). Local
solutions starting sufficiently near the limiting tuple exist in disks of a common positive radius.
Starting at an interior point sufficiently close to the boundary, uniqueness identifies that solution
with the actual slice on their overlap. This continues the whole slice arc across the chosen boundary
point.
    It remains to explain why an extension of this single slice yields a joint extension, as required
for Theorem 4.9. We give the argument at the upper boundary. The states

                                               Z = (s0 , s1 )

on the extended arc form a regular analytic curve, parameterized by y. All incidence angles and
chords along its orbit are nondegenerate. Thus the local billiard return R, after N reflections and
subtraction of the known 2π shifts, is analytic near these states. At each reflection, this follows by
solving the next incidence equation on the regular table arc; its derivative in the next intersection
coordinate is nonzero because the sine of incidence is nonzero. The return fixes the extended curve,
by analytic identity from below.
    The determinant of its linearization at a fixed state is one. One can see this without choosing
symplectic coordinates: successive linearized stationarity equations in the Jacobi variables have
transfer determinants equal to ratios of consecutive off-diagonal entries. The ratios telescope over the
cycle. The coordinate factors converting δsj to c sin d0j fj−2 δsj are also periodic along the fixed orbit.
Choose local coordinates (u, v) with the fixed arc given by (u, v) = (y, 0). The return consequently
has the form
                        R(u, v) = u + σ(u)v + O(v 2 ), v + τ (u)v 2 + O(v 3 ) .
                                                                                
                                                                                                   (10.14)
   In a lower part of this neighborhood the original joint data are known. For
                                                         
                           Z(y, t) = s(y, t), s(y + t, t) ,     b = N (t − t0 ),

the reflection identities give R(Z(y, t)) = Z(y + b, t). Expanding there in the coordinates just chosen,
write
                                 u = y + O(b),       v = bp0 (y) + O(b2 ).
The first component of the return identity at order b, and its second component at order b2 , give
respectively
                              σp0 = 1,      p′0 = τ p20 , σ ′ = −τ.                       (10.15)

                                                   123
These identities continue along the extended arc. By moving the boundary point slightly within
this arc if necessary, take a smaller neighborhood where σ ̸= 0, and set p0 = 1/σ.
    For small nonzero b with |ℑb| ≥ |b|/3, the return and its inverse continue the joint states
upwards in steps ±b. Here are the relevant uniform estimates. In the coordinates (u, p) = (u, v/b),
Equation (10.14) reads
                              (u, p) 7−→ (u, p) + b σ(u)p, τ (u)p2 + O(b2 )
                                                                  

on fixed compact subsets; the inverse has the negative first increment. The analytic differential
equation with that vector field has the regular solution (u, p) = (y, p0 (y)), by Equation (10.15).
Choose the arc neighborhood and the height to be crossed sufficiently small. The steepness condition
keeps all the slanted paths from the lower starting slab inside that neighborhood. There are O(1/|b|)
steps; each has error O(|b|2 ) and stability factor at most 1 + C|b|. The continued states therefore
stay within O(|b|) of this regular solution. All shape coordinates remain strictly inside the shape
strip.
    All intermediate site coordinates are recovered by the regular analytic billiard steps along the
orbit. Thus their continuations agree with the original sj ’s wherever the latter were already defined.
    Use locally constant numbers of backward steps to place initial states in the lower slab. On
overlaps, the exact return identity in that slab and uniqueness of each forward step identify
the constructions. They give joint holomorphic germs in the steep sectors, attached by vertical
continuation to the lower data.
    Finally choose a small η > 0, then a much smaller r > 0, and use the analytic disks

                        t = t0 + rζ,     y = x∗ + iς − iηζ 2 + w,     |ζ| ≤ 1,                 (10.16)

as ς rises from h − 2η to h; w varies in a sufficiently small complex disk. On the boundary arcs
where ζ is near the real axis, the phase point is a fixed amount below the wall. For small r, these
arcs lie in the original joint tube, by lower semicontinuity of the width. On the remaining arcs
the parameter is in a fixed steep sector, so the preceding continuation applies. The collar germs
agree because both are vertical continuations of the lower data. The full disks are initially inside
known data. Holomorphic product continuation, as in Theorem 4.6, fills them as the center is raised.
Equivalently, pull the germs back to a collar of the unit circle and a complex neighborhood of the
center path: the negative Cauchy moments vanish in the initial open part and hence throughout
that connected neighborhood. More explicitly, combine x∗ + iς + w into one complex center variable
a. The map (a, ζ) 7→ (a − iηζ 2 , t0 + rζ) has Jacobian determinant r ̸= 0, so the filling supplies a
joint neighborhood in (y, t).
    The maximum principle preserves |ℑsj | < H on the filled disks. Composition with Γ gives a
joint meromorphic patch for X at (x∗ + ih, t0 ), agreeing with the original tube below the wall. This
contradicts Theorem 4.9, which applies also to the rational parameter t0 . The lower boundary is
treated in the same way, or by real symmetry. The supposed positive-measure set therefore cannot
exist.

10.3    Brownian motion, history levels, and fixed stopping rules
We use the standard conformal time-change, optional-stopping, and martingale maximal theorems
for Brownian motion; see [RY99, Chapters II, IV and V]. Their applications, including the stopping
cuts, are specified below. Let yu be planar Brownian motion, started with y0 uniform on one real
period, and let ζ be its first exit from |ℑy| < h. All functions are evaluated on the real lift of this




                                                 124
path. Put

      δj (u) = H − |ℑsj (yu )|,    kj (u) = − log δj (u), Kj (u) = max kj (v),
                                                                                0≤v≤u
                                                                                                      (10.17)
      ry (u) = h − |ℑyu |,         ny (u) = − log ry (u),             τL = inf{u : max Kj (u) = L}.
                                                                                        j

For sufficiently large L, τL < ζ almost surely. Indeed the bounded functions eisj are Poisson integrals
of their almost everywhere boundary values. This follows first on narrower strips and then on the
whole strip by dominated convergence. Along Brownian motion they are the conditional-expectation
martingales of their terminal boundary values. Announcing the exit by exits from narrower strips
shows that their limits at Brownian exit are those terminal values. The exit distribution is absolutely
continuous on the horizontal boundary, so Theorem 10.3 makes at least one δj tend to zero.
Continuity then gives every sufficiently large history level before exit.
    The isolated zeros and poles of the meromorphic slice functions are polar for planar Brownian
motion and are almost surely avoided. All statements involving them can first be stopped on
compact subsets avoiding these points, followed by an exhaustion. In what follows, “with probability
tending to one through τL ” means that one event of probability 1 − o(1) supports the stated bounds
simultaneously at every time 0 ≤ u ≤ τL and at every site.

Lemma 10.4 (Shape time change and its consequences). Each process sj (yu ), before phase exit, is
a Brownian motion in the shape strip under the scalar time change

  (i) The expected occupation of any nonnegative periodic shape observable, integrated against dQj ,

       at clock time T > 0. This estimate also holds conditionally at that hit whenever it occurs.
 (iv) For a sufficiently large fixed C, with probability tending to one through τL ,

                                       kj (u) ≥ Kj (u) − C log L            for every j.              (10.20)

Proof. The real and imaginary parts of a holomorphic function of planar Brownian motion are local
martingales. The Cauchy–Riemann equations give equal quadratic variations dQj and zero mutual
quadratic covariation. The Brownian time-change theorem therefore applies. To use it up to a given

                                                        125
interior phase stop, append independent Brownian increments in intrinsic time after that stop. The
extension cannot have exited the shape strip before the part of the path already used, because all
those states lie in the strip. Approximation by compact interior stops proves the same domination
for nonnegative occupations over the whole phase interval.
    On the real collar y 7→ sj (y) is an increasing analytic circle diffeomorphism whose derivative is
bounded above and below. This proves the initial-density assertion. Under a uniform real start, the
periodic shape-strip Green weight is a constant times δ = H − |ℑs|. It has finite integral, proving
the total-clock claim. At either wall, the contribution of |f |2 up to log depth K is bounded by a
constant times            Z δ∗               Z     K            Z         K
                                  δ E|f |2 dδ =        δ 2 E|f |2 dk =        µf (k)2 dk.
                            e−K                   k∗                     k∗
The fixed interior region has bounded contribution. Now use Equation (10.1). Domination of
first-hit laws follows from the same Brownian extension; if the original path never reaches the row,
it contributes no mass.
     Starting at distance δ from one horizontal wall, the exit time from the strip is no greater than the
first time the vertical Brownian coordinate hits that  √ particular wall. The one-dimensional reflection
principle bounds survival to time T by C min(1, δ/ T ). Applying this after the hit, with the strong
Markov property in intrinsic time, proves (iii).
     For (iv), at the first hit of each integer level m, the probability of reaching depth Re−m before
hitting the adjacent wall is at most 1/R, by the linear one-dimensional hitting probability. Take R
a sufficiently large fixed power of L, and sum over the O(L) integer levels up to L, and the fixed
number of sites. If a retreat larger than C log L occurred, it would contain one of these integer-level
retreats, with an immaterial factor from rounding. The union probability tends to zero.

   We record explicitly the two localization principles used below.
Lemma 10.5 (Logarithmic deficit at a stopping cut). Let g be a nontrivial meromorphic slice
function, with log |g(y0 )| ≥ −C0 on the real starting row. Suppose log |g(yu )| ≤ B0 , where B0 ≥ 1,
up to a specified stopping cut T . Then
                                                                 B0 + C0   C(1 + B0 )
                                                        
                        P       inf log |g(yu )| < −B1       ≤           ≤                            (10.21)
                                u≤T                              B0 + B1    1 + B1
for B1 ≥ 1. Earlier bounds may be incorporated by stopping at their first failure; their failure
probabilities are then added separately.
Proof. Away from the polar zero and pole set, log |g(yu )| is a continuous local martingale. Before
the indicated cut, B0 − log |g(yu )| is nonnegative, hence is a supermartingale after localization.
Stop additionally at its first hit of B0 + B1 . The supermartingale inequality gives the first bound.
Exhaustion removes the auxiliary compact localizations.

    For an analytic differential integrated after a stopping time, we shall likewise apply the maximal
inequality only after a quadratic-time cut. More precisely, if its scalar quadratic time is at most a
deterministic B on some collection of paths, stop the integral at quadratic time 2B before estimating
its maximum. The resulting second-moment bound is O(B). It applies to the original integral on
those paths, without conditioning on the collection of paths. This remains true when the integrand
is masked by adapted status indicators, using their predictable versions. All ensuing probability
estimates use these cuts.

Lemma 10.6 (A finite family of modified inverse estimates). Let V ≥ U ≥ 1 be deterministic. For
each fixed proper truthful set I and flat set F ⊆ I c , stop at τL , at the first failure of G ≤ U , or at the
first time an I-history exceeds U , whichever comes first. Except on an event of probability at most
                                                 C(1 + U )
                                                           ,                                          (10.24)
                                                  1+V
the bound
                                              −1
                                            ∥BI,F ∥ ≤ exp(CV )                                        (10.25)
holds throughout this stopped interval. The constant is uniform over the finitely many choices of
I, F . Thus one may secure all these bounds by a finite union, and subsequently choose I, F as
functions of the current state before the common geometry cut, whenever their selected history caps
hold.
Proof. On the stopped interval, Equation (10.23) bounds ai by C(1 + U ) for i ∈ I. The geometry
cap bounds the reciprocal lengths, the reciprocal incidence sines, and the angles. Hence every entry,
determinant, and cofactor of BI,F has modulus at most exp(C(1 + U )). There is no amplitude
bound required at flat or deleted sites: their curvature terms do not appear. By Theorem 10.1,
the determinant has a positive minimum in modulus on the compact real starting period. Apply
Theorem 10.5 to that determinant with upper bound C(1 + U ) and lower-log threshold −V . The
adjugate formula gives Equation (10.25) outside the resulting exceptional event. Empty matrices
require no estimate.
    There are at most 3N choices. Each matrix is defined and estimated from the original real start,
with its own fixed stopping rule. If at a later time all the histories selected by that matrix are at
most U , none of them has crossed its cap previously. Thus the estimate already secured for that
fixed candidate is still valid as long as the common geometry cut has not occurred. This is the
reason for using history levels rather than current depths. No estimate is restarted when the current
selection changes.

hysteresis gap is bG. By Equation (10.23), CL − aj is a nonnegative local martingale, hence a
supermartingale, up to τL and the preceding cuts. Its upcrossing levels are CL + bG and CL + 2bG.
The upcrossing inequality, and alternation of the two switch directions, give an expected number of
switches O(L/G). One can also obtain this bound by holding supermartingale increments during
attempted passages between the two levels: each completed passage earns bG, while an unfinished
passage has possible loss bounded by CL + 2bG. Thus, for any sufficiently small fixed ε1 > 0, the
total number of switches over the fixed number of sites is at most

The purpose of the first part of the section is to find a deterministic shape row on which almost

in the odd Jacobi system, but by different identities. The final argument applies this suppression to
the last site to cross the tested row.

11.1    A discrete occupation estimate
We first isolate the probabilistic estimate needed when the row moments are small. It does not
require a bound on the starting value of the submartingale.

11.2    The large-moment alternative
Lemma 11.2. Suppose that, along a sequence K → ∞, some j ∈ [0.9K, 2.1K] satisfies µ(j) ≥ K α .
There is a deterministic k ∈ [0.8K, 2.2K] for which
                     1 n                                               o
                        x ∈ [0, 2π] : log |f (x + i(H − e−k ))| ≤ K α/2 −→ 0.                  (11.5)
                    2π
Proof. Choose a small fixed c > 0, with c < 1/4, and maximize µ(l)e−c|l−j| over l ≥ k0 . The
maximum exists by (11.1). A maximizer k satisfies

                  |k − j| = O(log K),        B := µ(k) ≥ K α ,          µ(l) ≤ Bec|l−k| .

For a uniform random center x, use the normalized profiles

                            dk                                         d2k
               FK,x (Z) =      f (x + iH + dk Z),         QK,x (Z) =       q(x + iH + dk Z).   (11.6)
                            B                                          B2
Their domains exhaust the lower half-plane. At a depth in the fixed upper collar, 0 < D ≤ ek−k0 ,
the moment envelope gives

                     E |FK,x (X − iD)|2 + |QK,x (X − iD)| ≤ CD−2 e2c| log D| .
                                                                  
                                                                                               (11.7)

The bound is uniform in the horizontal coordinate X. Submean estimates on fixed disks give
tightness in the topology of locally uniform convergence. More explicitly, take a countable compact
exhaustion, view the restrictions in the product of the corresponding continuous-function spaces,
and put a zero placeholder on compact sets not yet contained in a domain. Tightness and diagonal
extraction give consistent holomorphic limits. Uniformity of the original center makes each limiting
law stationary under all real translations.
    The scaled equation is
                                           ′′
                                         FK,x = B 2 QK,x FK,x .
Consequently each limit (F, Q) is pure: F Q = 0, so at least one of the two holomorphic functions
vanishes identically. The limit law has no atom at (0, 0). To check precisely the extra hypothesis

                                                    133
needed for Theorem 8.6, choose 1/2 < η < 1 − c. In a fixed enlarged cone, subdivide the coarser
depths 1 ≤ D ≤ δb /dk into dyadic bands. The choice of δb in Section 8 keeps even the fixed tile
enlargements within dk D ≤ e−k0 , the range of (11.7). Each band contains only a bounded number
of tiles of size comparable to its depth. By (11.7) and submean, the second moments of
                                                       q
                                        Dη |FK,x | +
                                                                     
                                                            |QK,x |

on these tiles have a summable bound CD−2(1−c−η) . Thus the required cone bound has a tight
random constant. Positive mass at the trivial pair would allow selection of centers converging to
that pair while this constant stayed bounded: first discard a set of probability smaller than half the
alleged atom, then use successively smaller compact neighborhoods of zero. The moment envelope
has coarser exponent c < 1/2 and finer exponent c > 0, so this selection contradicts Theorem 8.6.
    There is one more degeneracy to exclude. With probability one, a nonzero limiting Q is not real
on the entire line ℑZ = −1. Otherwise condition on the event that it is real there and nonzero; this
event is translation invariant. Schwarz reflection across that line extends Q to an entire function.
For each fixed D ≥ 1, (11.7) holds for all sufficiently large k, so Fatou’s lemma gives
                                      E|Q(X − iD)| ≤ CD−2(1−c) .
Conditioning increases the constant only by the reciprocal probability of the event. Reflection
supplies the same bound in the reflected half-plane. Every point is represented either at depth at
least one or by its reflection at such a depth; hence the conditional mean modulus is bounded on
the entire plane. Cauchy’s derivative estimate on circles of radius R, followed by expectation, gives
E|Q′ (z)| ≤ C/R. Letting R → ∞ makes Q constant almost surely; the decay at large depth makes
that constant zero. This contradicts the event on which we conditioned.
    We now examine any deterministic convergent sequence of profiles with one of the allowed limits.
If F ̸≡ 0, then, away from its discrete zeros on a fixed row segment,
                         log |f (x + iH + dk Z)| = k + log B + log |FK,x (Z)|.
The right side exceeds K α/2 uniformly on compact subsets away from those zeros. Arbitrarily small
neighborhoods of the zeros therefore contain all the exceptional points in the limit.
    Suppose instead that Q ̸≡ 0. Since its imaginary part on ℑZ = −1 is a nonzero real-analytic
function, its zeros on compact row intervals are discrete. Apart from arbitrarily small neighborhoods
of these points, cover the row
                            p
                               by finitely many short intervals with complex neighborhoods on which
consistent branches pK = QK,x satisfy
                                               ℜpK ≥ c1 > 0
and have uniformly bounded derivatives. In the scaled variable the original function g(Z) =
f (x + iH + dk Z) satisfies g ′′ = B 2 QK,x g. A basis on each such interval has the form
                                                      Z u                  
                g± (u) = QK,x (u − i)−1/4 exp ±B                pK (v − i) dv (1 + O(B −1 )).   (11.8)
                                                           u0

Here and below the error is uniform on the chosen closed interval. For completeness, the expressions
without the final factor solve the same equation with an added uniformly bounded potential; their
Wronskian has modulus comparable to B. Variation of constants, from the left for the growing
expression and from the right for the decaying expression, gives Volterra equations for the relative
factors. After division by the leading expressions the nonconstant kernel contains a factor
                                         Z u                    
                              exp −2B           pK (r − i) dr ,          v ≤ u,
                                           v


                                                     134
whose modulus is at most e−2Bc1 (u−v) ; the remaining kernel has size O(B −1 ). Successive approxima-
tion, or the integral Gronwall inequality, therefore gives relative error O(B −1 ). The two solutions
are independent, since their modulus ratio changes by an exponential factor along the interval. This
proves (11.8).
    Write g = A+ g+ + A− g− , with coefficients allowed to depend on K. If both coefficients
are nonzero, the difference of the two leading logarithmic magnitudes      √ has derivative at −1/2
                                                                                                 least
2c1 B − O(1). The set on which that difference has √modulus at most B has length O(B                 ).
Outside it one term dominates the other by at least e B+O(1) . Each individual leading logarithmic
magnitude is strictly monotone with slope of absolute value at least c1 B − O(1), so it spends at
most O((K α/2 + 1)/B) = o(1) length within K α/2 + 1 of zero. The same conclusion holds when one
coefficient vanishes. Thus the fraction violating (11.5) on every fixed scaled interval tends to zero.
    The countable product of continuous-function spaces used above is Polish. By the Skorokhod
representation theorem [Pol02, Chapter 10, Theorem 4, p. 239], any weakly convergent subsequence
of profile laws admits an almost-sure coupling. The deterministic criterion therefore applies almost
surely. The local bad fraction is bounded by one, so its expectation tends to zero. Translation
averaging identifies this expectation with the full-period bad fraction on the selected row. Every
subsequence has the same conclusion, proving the lemma.

11.3    Amplitude control when the row moments are small
Assume for the remainder of this subsection that

                                 µ(v) ≤ K α      (0.9K ≤ v ≤ 2.1K).                                  (11.9)

For a shape point z, write

                                                              A(z) = log |f (z)| + δ(z)|f ′ (z)| .
                                                                                                
            δ(z) = H − |ℑz|,      a(z) = log |f (z)|,

The function A is finite and continuous: a common zero of f and f ′ would imply f ≡ 0 by the
equation f ′′ = qf . In contrast, a has logarithmic singularities at the isolated zeros of f . The next
estimate keeps these two issues separate.

Lemma 11.3 (Local amplitude and logarithmic deficit). Let z0 = x + i(H − d), d < H/4, and put
                                                               q
                                      Hb = 1 +     sup        δ |q|.
                                                 B(z0 ,d/2)


On B(z0 , d/8), the oscillation of A is at most CHb . Moreover, the normalized area L2 norm of
A − a on that disk, and its normalized length L2 norm on the horizontal diameter, are at most CHb .
The same estimates hold for fixed smaller disks and for horizontal segments of length comparable to
d with fixed interior margins.

Proof. Scale by d/8, writing

                     F (ζ) = f (z0 + (d/8)ζ),      Q(ζ) = (d/8)2 q(z0 + (d/8)ζ).

These functions are holomorphic on |ζ| < 4, and |Q|1/2 ≤ Hb /4. The nonvanishing vector Y =
(F, F ′ /Hb ) satisfies                              !
                                      ′      0    Hb
                                    Y =                Y.

                                                  135
The matrix norm is at most CHb . Gronwall along a straight segment, used in both directions, bounds
the change of log ∥Y ∥ by CHb on fixed interior disks. Since physical depth there is comparable to d,
replacing log ∥Y ∥ by A changes the oscillation by at most C log(2 + Hb ). In particular,

                             |A(z) − A(z0 )| ≤ CHb             (|z − z0 | ≤ 3d/8).                  (11.10)

   Set A0 = A(z0 ) and G = e−A0 F . Cauchy’s estimate on a sufficiently small fixed circle shows
that there is a point |ζ∗ | ≤ 1/16 with log |G(ζ∗ )| ≥ −C: indeed a fixed multiple of sup|ζ|≤1/16 |F (ζ)|
bounds |F (0)| + 8|F ′ (0)| = eA0 . On |ζ| ≤ 3, (11.10) gives log |G| ≤ CHb . Choose M = C0 Hb large
enough and set U = M − log |G| ≥ 0. Then U (ζ∗ ) ≤ CHb .
   Jensen’s formula on a circle centered at ζ∗ , of radius in [2, 9/4] avoiding boundary zeros, bounds
by CHb the number of zeros in B(ζ∗ , 7/4), with multiplicities. Choose a further radius in [3/2, 7/4]
avoiding boundary zeros. The boundary mean of U is at most U (ζ∗ ), by submean for log |G|.
Poisson–Jensen expresses U inside this latter circle as the harmonic extension of that nonnegative
boundary data plus the positive disk Green functions of the zeros. On |ζ| ≤ 1, the harmonic term is
bounded by CHb . Each Green function is bounded there by

                                               C + log− |ζ − ζj |,

which has uniformly bounded L2 norm on the unit disk and on its horizontal diameter. For the
segment assertion use log− |t + ib − ζj | ≤ log− |t − ℜζj | and the integrability of | log |t||2 . There are
at most CHb zeros, so the triangle inequality bounds the required L2 norms of U by CHb . Finally,

                                      A − a = (A − A0 − M ) + U,

and (11.10) bounds the other summand. Fixed smaller disks and comparable segments follow by
the same fixed ratios, or by a finite covering.

   The tile quantities in Theorem 11.3 obey

                  Ex Hb (x, dv )2 ≤ CK 2α ,           Ex (A − a)2 (x + i(H − dv )) ≤ CK 2α ,        (11.11)

whenever the fixed enlargement stays within the band (11.9). To see the first assertion, submean
for |q|, followed by averaging in the center, gives
                                            Z 7dv /4                             !
                     Ex Hb2 ≤ C    1 + dv              Ex |q(x + i(H − t))| dt       ≤ CK 2α .

The second follows by integrating the segment estimate of Theorem 11.3 in its center. Larger fixed
tiles or apertures cost only a fixed covering factor. The lower row follows by symmetry.
     Run ordinary Brownian motion in the shape strip from a uniform point of its real period. At the
first hit of either row of depth e−v , write a(v) for the value of a. These variables, sampled on any
increasing finite grid, form an integrable submartingale. Indeed apply subharmonic comparison in
the next substrip to max{log |f |, −T } and then let T → ∞; row logarithms are integrable, including
at isolated zeros. Translation invariance makes the horizontal coordinate uniform at each sample,
and symmetry makes the two sides equiprobable.




                                                        136
Lemma 11.4 (Logarithmic increments). Under (11.9), uniformly for 1.1K ≤ v ≤ 1.9K and
2 ≤ s ≤ 4,
                             E|a(v + s) − a(v)|2 ≤ CK 2α .                     (11.12)
Proof. Start on the upper row of depth d = e−v and stop on first hitting either row of depth
d′ = de−s . Let U be the unwrapped horizontal displacement. The distance to the upper target line
is d − d′ ≍ d. Ignoring the lower target line gives half-plane exit with Cauchy density
                                              d − d′
                                                             .
                                         π((d − d′ )2 + u2 )
Consequently

               P(same side, |U | > Rd) ≤ C/R     (R ≥ 1),        P(opposite side) ≤ Cd.      (11.13)

The second estimate is also the elementary linear hitting probability between two horizontal lines.
Conditional on displacement and on both sides, the starting horizontal point is still uniform modulo
2π; the terminal point is its translate, hence uniform as well. This follows directly by translating
the Brownian path and its start.
     For a same-side displacement u with R = |u|/d ≤ eK/20 , join the endpoints by a prescribed
path going first to depth D = d + |u|, then horizontally by u, and then to depth d′ . Subdivide the
vertical pieces in fixed small relative steps. The horizontal piece requires only a bounded number of
depth-sized tiles. The whole chain has at most C(1 + log(1 + R)) tiles. All their fixed enlargements
lie in the low-moment band for large K. At fixed u, their centers are fixed translates of the uniform
starting point. By Theorem 11.3, (11.11), and Minkowski,

                      ∥A(end) − A(start)∥L2 (dx/(2π)) ≤ CK α (1 + log(1 + R)).

The square of the last factor has bounded expectation by (11.13).
    For all remaining transitions, compare amplitudes by a chain from each endpoint to a fixed
real collar, connecting there modulo period. The global bound (11.1) makes the sum of the tile
costs at most C(1 + K)3 : there are O(K) levels and the cost at level j is O((1 + j)2 ). Thus the
amplitude difference is pointwise polynomially bounded even when an endpoint is near a zero of f .
The remaining transitions have probability at most C(e−K/20 + e−v ), by (11.13). They contribute
o(1) to its second moment. We have proved

                                  E|A(end) − A(start)|2 ≤ CK 2α .

Finally subtract A − a at both endpoints. Their uniform horizontal marginal laws and (11.11) bound
each squared error by CK 2α . This proves (11.12). Only the amplitude difference was bounded
pointwise on rare transitions; no pointwise bound for the logarithmic singularities is needed.

Theorem 11.6 (Deterministic row sign test). For every sufficiently large K, there is a deterministic
k = k(K) ∈ [0.8K, 2.2K] such that, on each shape row of depth e−k ,

                                             | log |f || > K α/2                                  (11.23)

outside a set of relative horizontal measure tending to zero as K → ∞.

Proof. If µ(j) ≥ K α for some j ∈ [0.9K, 2.1K], use Theorem 11.2. Otherwise (11.9) holds, and
Theorem 11.5 provides a deterministic row in [1.2K, 1.8K] with exceptional fraction tending to zero
for the stronger threshold h0 /4. Since K α/2 = o(h0 ), it also gives (11.23). Real symmetry gives the
lower-row conclusion from the upper one. The sequential proofs imply the stated conclusion as
K → ∞, without restricting to either alternative along a fixed subsequence.

11.5    Robustness of the sign test
Proposition 11.7 (A robust sign disk). For the deterministic level in Theorem 11.6, put

                                  h1 = 21 K α/2 ,          rrob = e−k−h1 /4 .                     (11.24)

Outside a set of relative horizontal measure tending to zero on either tested row, each point z has

                                 | log |f (ζ)|| ≥ h1           (|ζ − z| ≤ rrob ),

with constant sign on that disk. In the odd-slice Brownian experiment, for each fixed site the
probability that its first hit of the tested shape depth occurs at a point without this property tends to
zero.

Proof. Write d = e−k . By (11.1), |q| ≤ Cd−2 K 4 throughout a fixed depth-sized neighborhood of
either tested row. Zeros of f in this neighborhood are separated by at least cdK −2 . Indeed, scale
around a zero by r = cdK −2 , choosing c small. The scaled potential has arbitrarily small supremum
on a fixed disk, and the solution normalized to have derivative one and value zero at the center
satisfies                                   Z       ζ
                                 F (ζ) = ζ +            (ζ − u)Q(u)F (u) du.
                                                0

The integral equation gives |F (ζ) − ζ| ≤ C∥Q∥∞ |ζ|3 on a smaller fixed disk. Thus the zero is the
only one there. This also reproves simplicity in this setting.
    Packing disks of radius comparable to dK −2 in a periodic band of width O(d) bounds its number
of zeros by Cd−1 K 4 . Delete from the tested row the shadows of disks of radius

                                             rexc = de−h1 /10

about all these zeros. Their total relative length is at most CK 4 e−h1 /10 = o(1). Zeros outside this
band are too far away to affect the following smaller disks.
   At a remaining point, and throughout its disk of radius rrob ,

                                               f′

Here is the local zero test proving this bound. If λ = f ′ (z)/f (z) has modulus much larger than rexc−1 ,

rescale by 1/λ and divide by f (z). On |u| ≤ 2 the resulting equation has arbitrarily small potential,
because sup |q|/|λ|2 ≤ CK 4 e−h1 /5 , and its initial data are F (0) = F ′ (0) = 1. The integral equation

therefore o(1), including paths on which the site is stopped before reaching that row.

11.6    The last moving site
Return to the odd periodic phase slice of Theorems 10.1 and 10.9. The odd integer N and the real
parameter t0 = 2π/N are fixed. Brownian time in the phase strip is denoted by u, so derivatives s′j
always mean derivatives with respect to the complex phase coordinate y, not derivatives in time.
Retain the notation

     aj = log |f (sj )|,   kj = − log(H − |ℑsj |),         Kj (u) = sup kj (v),   wmax = max |wj |.
                                                                     v≤u                   j


The stopping time τL is the first time one of the histories Kj reaches L. By Theorem 10.3, it occurs
almost surely before phase exit for all large L.

Lemma 11.8 (A deterministic history gap). There is a fixed finite menu of exponents 0 < γm < 1
such that for each large L, one deterministic choice K = Lγm has the following event of probability
greater than 0.9: every terminal history Kj (τL ) is either at most K α/8 or at least 3K. At least one
history is in the second class.

Proof. Choose a fixed integer M > 10N . Choose positive exponents γ1 < · · · < γM < 1, small
enough and separated so that γm < γm+1 α/8. Then the intervals

                                  (Lγm α/8 , 3Lγm ),       1 ≤ m ≤ M,

are pairwise disjoint and lie below L, for all large L. Each path has at most N failed menu choices:
a single terminal history belongs to at most one of the intervals. Averaging failure probabilities over
the menu gives a deterministic choice with failure probability at most N/M < 0.1. One history
equals L at τL , so the large class is nonempty. Because the menu is fixed and finite, all subsequent
o(1) estimates can be made uniform over its choices.

   Fix this deterministic K, and set

                                  u0 = K α/8 ,         h2 = K α/4 = u20 .                        (11.26)

On the event of Theorem 11.8, denote the shallow indices by I and their nonempty complement by
J. These sets depend on the terminal path. In the estimates below, however, every candidate set is
fixed before a stopping argument is applied.

                                                         142
Theorem 11.10 (The last-site contradiction). The nonisotropic finite critical-wall case is impossible.

Proof. Use Theorem 11.6 for the deterministic K just chosen, and let k ∈ [0.8K, 2.2K] be its
deterministic tested level. Write d = e−k , and retain h1 = K α/2 /2 and rrob = de−h1 /4 from
Theorem 11.7. Notice the strict separation

and does not depend on later membership of j in a classification set.

Figure 5: The last-site contradiction, drawn without metric scale. For a fixed candidate set, TJ is

This is an unconditional martingale estimate for each fixed (J, i). The classification event, last-hit
identity, geometry bounds, and robustness are intersected with it only afterward. Ties among last
sites cause no problem: choose any tied index and include it in the same finite union.
    Disk exit is nevertheless compulsory on the classification event. An actual last site has depth d
at TJ = σi , and subsequently attains depth at most e−3K by τL . It cannot have attained that depth
earlier, since that would already have crossed the tested row. The depth function is 1-Lipschitz, so
its displacement before τL is at least

                                          d − e−3K ≥ d/2 > rrob

for large K. Thus it must leave the disk shown in Figure 5. The finite union of (11.36) over all
candidates J, i makes this event have probability o(1), whereas Theorem 11.8 and all the excluded
failure estimates give probability at least 0.9 − o(1). This contradiction proves the theorem.

11.7    Completion of the rigidity theorem
Proof of Theorem 1.2. The physical caustic-collar hypothesis gives an analytic boundary by Theo-
rem 2.1, and then the even joint parametrization and nonzero area coefficient of Theorem 3.1. The
continuation and no-patch theorems produce the maximal shape strip DH , with the natural-boundary
conclusion of Theorem 4.10 when H < ∞.
    If the meromorphic normal a = eiϕ has a zero or pole inside this strip, Theorem 5.3 already
identifies the boundary as an ellipse. In the remaining case, Theorem 5.4 and Theorem 5.5 give the
holomorphic shape fields and the intrinsic impact lift used above. Constant f gives a circle. For
nonconstant f , Theorem 7.7 excludes both H = ∞ and infinite upper growth order at a finite wall.
At a finite wall, Theorem 6.6 excludes upper growth order below one, and Theorem 6.8 excludes
every finite upper growth order above one. The only remaining possibility is a finite critical wall.
The critical bootstrap, Theorem 9.1, and the odd-slice construction place it under the hypotheses of
Theorem 11.10, which excludes it as well.

                                                      144
   Only an elliptic boundary, including a circle, remains. Convexity then identifies the domain
with the interior of that ellipse. After translation, it is therefore described by a positive definite
quadratic form, as asserted.
